Good — I pulled apart your starter repo and the A2 form/spec. Here's where you stand and exactly what's left, mapped to your group's axes (non-english-bangla-conjunct-script-plus-degraded-scans + robust-and-reliable).

What A2 actually requires

Per pipeline.py, the fixed order is: load_pages → preprocess → enhance → layout.detect → ocr.transcribe → chunk.split → embed.encode → store.build. You've done the first two (loader.py, preprocess.py). Remaining, in build order:

#
File → function
What it does
1
ingest/enhance.py → Enhancer.apply()
Denoise/repair degraded scans (your E1 speciality — gated, see below)
2
vision/layout.py → detect()
Text/table/figure/heading region detection
3
vision/ocr.py → Reader.transcribe_region(), transcribe()
Bangla OCR (your E3 speciality)
4
index/chunk.py → split()
Chunking + Bangla orthography normalization (also E3)
5
index/embed.py → encode()
Embed chunks (multilingual model, since corpus is Bangla)
6
index/store.py → build(), load()
FAISS index persistence

Plus authored artifacts: scripts/build_index.sh, notebooks/kb_demo.ipynb, reports/pipeline_diagram.md, configs/design_choices.md (Stages 0–4 rows).

Your two gated ▲ data-speciality items (mandatory for you, not everyone)

E1 (degraded scans) → lives in ingest/preprocess.py (you may already partly cover this) + optionally ingest/enhance.py if scans are bad enough to need a trained denoiser. Given these are 1900s-era DLI archive scans, expect ink bleed-through, warping, low contrast — worth showing before/after samples in the notebook even if you keep enhancement classical (OpenCV: adaptive threshold, morphological cleanup) rather than training a VAE/diffusion model (that's bonus-tier, ➕).

E3 (non-English/conjunct script) → lives in vision/ocr.py (pick/fine-tune a script-tuned OCR) and index/chunk.py (Unicode normalization for Bangla conjuncts).

Note: your NFR robust-and-reliable (E34) is built in A3 (eval/robustness.py), not A2 — don't worry about it yet, but keep a "worst failure" note now (Section 5's table asks for it) since it'll motivate that A3 work.

Concrete model choices given your corpus

Since config.yaml currently defaults to English-oriented models (microsoft/trocr-base-printed, all-MiniLM-L6-v2), you need Bangla-appropriate swaps:

OCR: Tesseract with ben traineddata is the easiest reproducible baseline (satisfies Section 3's "≥1 reproduced published/pretrained method" requirement directly). If you want a stronger deep option, look at Bangla-fine-tuned TrOCR/Donut checkpoints on HF — but Tesseract-ben is safer to get working end-to-end first, then swap if time allows.

Embedding: swap all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (English-only) for a multilingual model — e.g. sentence-transformers/paraphrase-multilingual-mpnet-base-v2 or l3cube-pune/bengali-sentence-similarity-sbert if you find a Bangla-specific one. Update dim in config accordingly.

Chunking normalization: in index/chunk.py, before splitting, run Unicode NFC normalization on the OCR'd Bangla text — conjuncts (যুক্তাক্ষর) can come out of OCR/Tesseract in inconsistent composed/decomposed forms, which will silently hurt retrieval if left alone.

Suggested order of attack

vision/layout.py — even a simple heuristic (whole page = one text region, since these look like plain text scans, no complex multi-column layout) is defensible as your "baseline" choice — document why in Section 3.

vision/ocr.py — get Tesseract-ben running on a handful of pages first, eyeball output before wiring into the full pipeline.

ingest/enhance.py — only if OCR quality on raw preprocessed pages is bad; otherwise enabled: false in config and note that decision in Section 3/7 (still counts as a real design choice).

index/chunk.py, index/embed.py, index/store.py — straightforward once text is decent.

Run build_index.sh end-to-end, then build kb_demo.ipynb: show 2–3 raw pages next to their OCR output, report a rough CER/WER against your grading_kit/labels.jsonl ground truth (you said this exists — use it here), then run one real retrieval query and show the top chunk is the right page.

Fill configs/design_choices.md and reports/pipeline_diagram.md last, once choices are locked in.

Architected layout detection pipeline to filter figures before OCR processing.

Architected layout detection pipeline to filter figures before OCR processing.

Done

Good instinct — you handle this in vision/layout.py, not in the OCR stage. Region already carries a kind field (text | table | figure | heading from contracts.py), so the fix is: layout detection must correctly tag figure regions as kind="figure", and ocr.transcribe() must skip any region whose kind is "figure" before calling the Reader. That keeps your OCR quality metrics automatically consistent with labels.jsonl too, since you're not generating text for something your ground truth never covers.

Option A (recommended) — LayoutParser + pretrained PubLayNet

This also happens to satisfy Section 3's "≥1 reproduced published/pretrained method" requirement for the layout stage specifically, on top of OCR — good to mention in the form.

# vision/layout.py
from __future__ import annotations
import layoutparser as lp
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

_MODEL = None

def _get_model():
    global _MODEL
    if _MODEL is None:
        _MODEL = lp.Detectron2LayoutModel(
            "lp://PubLayNet/faster_rcnn_R_50_FPN_3x/config",
            extra_config=["MODEL.ROI_HEADS.SCORE_THRESH_TEST", 0.5],
            label_map={0: "Text", 1: "Title", 2: "List", 3: "Table", 4: "Figure"},
        )
    return _MODEL

_KIND_MAP = {"Text": "text", "Title": "heading", "List": "text", "Table": "table", "Figure": "figure"}

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    model = _get_model()
    thr = cfg["layout"]["score_thr"]
    regions: list[Region] = []
    for page in pages:
        image = lp.io.load_image(page.image_path)  # or cv2.imread + convert
        layout = model.detect(image)
        for block in layout:
            if block.score < thr:
                continue
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, block.coordinates)
            regions.append(Region(
                page_id=page.id,
                bbox=(x1, y1, x2, y2),
                kind=_KIND_MAP.get(block.type, "text"),
            ))
    return regions

pip install layoutparser "layoutparser[layoutmodels]" torch detectron2 — Detectron2 install can be finicky on Colab; if it fights you, use Option B instead and just note in Section 3 why you picked the lighter approach.

Option B — classical heuristic (no GPU/Detectron2 needed)

Figures on these old agricultural scans (crop/soil diagrams) tend to be one or two large solid connected blobs with high edge density and low "text periodicity," while text blocks are many small, evenly-spaced connected components forming horizontal lines. A cheap discriminator:

# vision/layout.py
from __future__ import annotations
import cv2
import numpy as np
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

def _candidate_boxes(gray: np.ndarray) -> list[tuple[int, int, int, int]]:
    # binarize, then dilate horizontally+vertically to merge chars into blocks
    _, bw = cv2.threshold(gray, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV | cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    kernel = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_RECT, (25, 15))
    dilated = cv2.dilate(bw, kernel, iterations=2)
    contours, _ = cv2.findContours(dilated, cv2.RETR_EXTERNAL, cv2.CHAIN_APPROX_SIMPLE)
    boxes = []
    for c in contours:
        x, y, w, h = cv2.boundingRect(c)
        if w * h > 0.005 * gray.shape[0] * gray.shape[1]:  # drop tiny noise
            boxes.append((x, y, x + w, y + h))
    return boxes

def _classify(gray: np.ndarray, box: tuple[int, int, int, int]) -> str:
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = box
    crop = gray[y1:y2, x1:x2]
    _, bw = cv2.threshold(crop, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV | cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    n_cc, _, stats, _ = cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(bw)
    if n_cc <= 1:
        return "figure"
    areas = stats[1:, cv2.CC_STAT_AREA]
    mean_area, std_area = areas.mean(), areas.std()
    cc_density = n_cc / (crop.shape[0] * crop.shape[1] / 1000)
    # text: many small, similarly-sized components; figure: few large/irregular ones
    if cc_density > 0.5 and std_area < mean_area * 2:
        return "text"
    return "figure"

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    regions: list[Region] = []
    for page in pages:
        gray = cv2.imread(page.image_path, cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE)
        for box in _candidate_boxes(gray):
            regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=box, kind=_classify(gray, box)))
    return regions

Tune the thresholds (0.005 area cutoff, cc_density/std_area split) on a handful of your pages — pull 5–6 pages you know contain figures and eyeball the boxes/labels before trusting it on all 749.

The part that actually skips OCR on figures

Regardless of which layout option you use, add the filter here:

# vision/ocr.py
def transcribe(regions: list[Region], cfg: dict) -> list[Chunk]:
    reader = Reader(cfg)
    chunks = []
    for r in regions:
        if r.kind == "figure":
            continue  # no OCR on figures — no ground truth, would be gibberish
        text = reader.transcribe_region(r)
        chunks.append(Chunk(id=..., doc_id=..., text=text, page_ids=[r.page_id]))
    return chunks

Worth noting in configs/design_choices.md (Stage 2 row) and Section 5's "worst failure" cell if the classifier misfires on some pages — that honesty is graded, not penalized.

Done

That traceback means the “config file” LayoutParser tried to load isn't actually YAML — it's HTML. LayoutParser pulls PubLayNet configs from a Dropbox link (iopath_cache/.../config.yml?dl=1), and Dropbox occasionally serves a redirect/error page instead of the raw file, which then gets cached and permanently breaks every future load until you clear it.

Quick fix, if you want to keep Detectron2/LayoutParser:

rm -rf ~/.torch/iopath_cache

Then rerun — it'll redownload. If it fails again the same way, the Dropbox link is likely rate-limiting you, and it'll keep happening.

But honestly, I'd switch to Option B (the classical OpenCV heuristic) instead of chasing this. Reasons specific to your setup:

You're on a Mac (.venv, no CUDA visible in the trace) — Detectron2 is a pain to keep working on macOS/Apple Silicon, and this Dropbox-config flakiness is a known recurring headache for exactly this combo.

Your corpus is fairly simple structurally (soil-science manuals — mostly running text with occasional diagrams, not dense multi-column academic layouts), so PubLayNet's extra sophistication isn't buying you much.

You already have Option B written and it doesn't depend on any network fetch at runtime — much safer for reproducibility on grading day (Section 5 wants a green scripts/build_index.sh run; a Dropbox hiccup mid-grading is exactly the kind of thing you don't want blocking you).

You still get the "≥1 reproduced pretrained method" requirement from your OCR stage (Tesseract-ben), so you're not losing that box.

If you go this route, just swap vision/layout.py for the OpenCV version I gave you earlier, note in configs/design_choices.md (Stage 2 row) that you tried Detectron2/PubLayNet first and moved to a classical heuristic because of environment fragility + corpus simplicity — that's a legitimate, gradeable design justification for Section 3's "options compared."

Want help tuning the _classify() thresholds against a few of your actual pages, or debugging further if you'd rather push through with Detectron2?

I used the openCV one in most cases it is misaligning the kind. the heading and texts are being detected as figures. how can I fix that

Assistant:

8/13/2026 0:50:51

Diagnosed script density misclassification; engineered periodicity-based detection solution.

Diagnosed script density misclassification; engineered periodicity-based detection solution.

Done

The root cause is specific to Bangla script: your _classify() relies on connected-component count/size to tell text from figures, but Bangla letters within a word are joined by the matra (শিরোরেখা) — the continuous horizontal head-stroke. After dilation, that turns each line (or even the whole paragraph) into one or a few giant connected components, which is exactly the "few large blobs" signature your heuristic uses to mean "figure." That's why dense text blocks and single-line headings are both getting mislabeled — the assumption behind that classifier was implicitly built for scripts without a connecting stroke.

The fix isn't to tweak thresholds — it's to swap the signal. Text pages, in any script, have a strongly periodic horizontal projection profile (each line of text creates a peak, evenly spaced by line height), while figures/photos don't. This sidesteps the matra problem entirely since it doesn't care how components merge within a line, only that lines repeat regularly.

# vision/layout.py
import numpy as np
import cv2

def _row_ink_profile(bw: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    return bw.sum(axis=1).astype(float)

def _periodicity_score(row_sums: np.ndarray, min_prominence_frac: float = 0.15):
    """Returns (n_peaks, coefficient_of_variation_of_spacing). Low CV + >=2 peaks = regular text lines."""
    if row_sums.max() == 0:
        return 0, 1.0
    # simple local-maxima peak finder (avoids needing scipy)
    thr = row_sums.max() * min_prominence_frac
    peaks = []
    i = 1
    while i < len(row_sums) - 1:
        if row_sums[i] > thr and row_sums[i] >= row_sums[i-1] and row_sums[i] >= row_sums[i+1]:
            peaks.append(i)
            i += 5  # skip ahead so we don't pick multiple points off one peak
        else:
            i += 1
    if len(peaks) < 2:
        return len(peaks), 1.0
    diffs = np.diff(peaks)
    cv = diffs.std() / (diffs.mean() + 1e-6)
    return len(peaks), cv

def _classify(gray: np.ndarray, box: tuple[int, int, int, int]) -> str:
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = box
    crop = gray[y1:y2, x1:x2]
    _, bw = cv2.threshold(crop, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV | cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    bw01 = (bw > 0).astype(np.uint8)

    row_sums = _row_ink_profile(bw01)
    n_peaks, cv = _periodicity_score(row_sums)

    if n_peaks >= 2 and cv < 0.35:
        return "text"          # multiple regularly-spaced line bands -> running text / paragraph

    # single-line case: headings, page titles, captions in boxes
    h, w = crop.shape
    aspect = w / max(h, 1)
    ink_density = bw01.mean()
    if n_peaks == 1 and aspect > 2.5 and ink_density < 0.35:
        return "text"          # wide, short, moderate density -> likely a heading/single text line

    return "figure"

Notes on why this handles your four samples specifically:

Image 3/4 (dense paragraph pages, entirely misclassified as figure): these will now show many strong, regularly-spaced peaks in the row profile (one per text line), so n_peaks >= 2 and low cv correctly routes them to "text" regardless of how the matra merges glyphs within each line.

Image 1's title bar / Image 4's page-number box ("figure" mislabels on headings): single-line, wide, low-density boxes now get caught by the fallback branch instead of falling through to "figure".

Image 2 (actual degraded photo scans): these have chaotic, non-periodic ink distribution (no line-spacing structure) and very high density — n_peaks stays low/irregular, so they correctly stay "figure".

Image 1's line-drawing figures: irregular ink distribution, no periodic horizontal bands → correctly "figure".

One tuning knob worth sanity-checking on your actual pages: min_prominence_frac=0.15 and the cv < 0.35 cutoff. Bangla body text with typical line spacing should pass comfortably, but if you have pages with very tight or very loose leading, pull 5–6 boxes (mix of headings, paragraphs, and real figures) and print (n_peaks, cv, aspect, ink_density) for each before trusting it on all 749 pages — same "eyeball first" approach as before.

Want to also revisit _candidate_boxes()'s dilation kernel? Right now (25,15) may still be merging separate elements (e.g. a heading box glued to the figure below it) before classification ever runs — worth checking on Image 1 and Image 4 specifically since both have adjacent figure+text regions close together.

Analyzed annotation overlay removal necessity for image processing pipeline.

Analyzed annotation overlay removal necessity for image processing pipeline.

Done

Good catch — and this actually reframes the real problem. Those red/green boxes with "figure"/"text" labels aren't part of the original scanned book. They're an annotation overlay — almost certainly something you (or a labeling tool) drew on top of the raw pages to build your ground truth for grading_kit/labels.jsonl. contracts.py's Region just stores bbox coordinates + a kind string as data — it was never asking for boxes to be burned into the pixels.

This matters a lot for your pipeline, for two separate reasons:

1. If data/raw/ actually contains these annotated images, that's a bug in your data pipeline, not your layout classifier. Two things go wrong:

OCR will try to read the literal word "figure"/"text" as page content, polluting your transcripts.

Layout detection will pick up the bright red/green rectangle borders as spurious edges/components — which is likely a real contributor to your misclassification, not just the matra issue from before.

2. The fix is upstream, not in layout.py: data/raw/ should hold the untouched scans; the boxes + kind labels belong only in grading_kit/labels.jsonl as coordinates, referenced by page id. Your labeling step (however you produced these overlay images) should be writing {page_id, bbox, kind} records to that JSONL, not saving a new annotated PNG as if it were the source page.

Can you check: when you open the actual files in data/raw/, do they have these colored boxes drawn on them, or is this overlay something that only appears in a separate visualization/export step (e.g. a debug image your labeling script produces alongside the clean one)? That answer changes what you need to fix.

If it turns out your raw scans genuinely got overwritten with annotated versions and you don't have clean copies anymore, you'd need to re-download from the Internet Archive source (scripts/get_data.sh) rather than trying to strip the overlay — color-based inpainting to remove red/green boxes is unreliable and risks damaging the text underneath them, especially right at box edges where captions sit.

If you do just want to detect/strip colored annotation pixels (e.g. to salvage images where you're sure nothing important was touched), OpenCV can isolate them since original scans are grayscale but the overlay is saturated color:

import cv2
import numpy as np

def strip_color_annotations(img_path: str) -> np.ndarray:
    img = cv2.imread(img_path)
    hsv = cv2.cvtColor(img, cv2.COLOR_BGR2HSV)
    # red and green have high saturation; original scan pixels are near-grayscale (low sat)
    sat = hsv[:, :, 1]
    mask = (sat > 60).astype(np.uint8) * 255
    mask = cv2.dilate(mask, np.ones((5, 5), np.uint8))  # cover box outline + text edges
    clean = cv2.inpaint(img, mask, 5, cv2.INPAINT_TELEA)
    return clean

But I'd treat this as a last resort, not the plan — re-pulling clean scans is safer and keeps your provenance story honest for data/provenance.md.

Ah — that clears it up. So data/raw/ is clean; debug_*.png is just your own visualization output. Good, that removes my earlier worry. The actual bug is in _classify() itself.

Looking at your images again with that context: paragraphs that do get labeled correctly (Image 1's caption) and paragraphs that don't (Image 3/4's dense text blocks, Image 1's bottom text) are inconsistent — which points to the periodicity heuristic being too fragile for real Bangla body text. Bangla vowel signs (কার) sit above and below the baseline and vary a lot in height line to line, so your row-ink profile doesn't produce the clean, evenly-spaced peaks the cv < 0.35 cutoff assumes. It's not wrong in principle, just brittle — hand-tuned pixel heuristics are exactly the kind of thing that looks fine on 2 test pages and falls apart on page 400.

Given you're already running Tesseract-ben for OCR, the more robust move is to let Tesseract itself tell you whether a region is text, instead of guessing from pixel geometry. Real text regions will yield many recognized words at reasonable confidence; figures will yield almost nothing (or garbage). This also removes an entire class of fragile heuristics from your report — one tool, one job.

# vision/layout.py
from __future__ import annotations
import cv2
import numpy as np
import pytesseract
from pytesseract import Output
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

def _candidate_boxes(gray: np.ndarray) -> list[tuple[int, int, int, int]]:
    _, bw = cv2.threshold(gray, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV | cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    kernel = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_RECT, (25, 15))
    dilated = cv2.dilate(bw, kernel, iterations=2)
    contours, _ = cv2.findContours(dilated, cv2.RETR_EXTERNAL, cv2.CHAIN_APPROX_SIMPLE)
    boxes = []
    for c in contours:
        x, y, w, h = cv2.boundingRect(c)
        if w * h > 0.005 * gray.shape[0] * gray.shape[1]:
            boxes.append((x, y, x + w, y + h))
    return boxes

def _classify(gray: np.ndarray, box: tuple[int, int, int, int], min_conf: float = 40.0, min_words: int = 3) -> str:
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = box
    crop = gray[y1:y2, x1:x2]
    data = pytesseract.image_to_data(crop, lang="ben", output_type=Output.DICT)

    confident_words = [
        w for w, c in zip(data["text"], data["conf"])
        if w.strip() and float(c) >= min_conf
    ]
    if len(confident_words) >= min_words:
        return "text"
    return "figure"

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    regions: list[Region] = []
    for page in pages:
        gray = cv2.imread(page.image_path, cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE)
        for box in _candidate_boxes(gray):
            regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=box, kind=_classify(gray, box)))
    return regions

Why this should fix all four of your failure cases:

Dense Bangla paragraphs (Image 3/4): will yield dozens of confidently-recognized words → "text", regardless of line-height irregularity.

Headings/title bars/single lines: even short lines usually yield ≥3 words at decent confidence.

Real figures (Image 1's flower diagrams, Image 2's photos): Tesseract either returns nothing or a handful of near-zero-confidence garbage tokens → correctly "figure".

Trade-off to be upfront about: this runs OCR twice per text region (once here, once again properly in vision/ocr.py), so it's slower — for 749 pages that's likely still fine, but if it's painfully slow you can cache image_to_data results here and reuse them in ocr.transcribe() instead of re-running Tesseract from scratch. Worth mentioning as a known trade-off in design_choices.md's Stage 2 row either way.

Tune min_conf/min_words by running visualize_regions() on a handful of pages first — same eyeball step as before. Want me to fold in a shared-cache version so Stage 2 and Stage 3 aren't duplicating OCR work?

8/13/2026 0:59:05

Honestly, no — it's a pragmatic patch, not the better option in principle. Worth being straight about the trade-off:

What the OCR-signal approach is actually good for: unblocking you right now without fighting Detectron2 on Windows/Mac. It's also legitimately defensible in your report — it's simple, has no external model dependency, and is easy to justify. But it has real weaknesses:

It conflates "text detection" with "text recognition." A region only gets called "text" if Tesseract can already read it — so on your worst, most degraded pages (which is precisely your data-speciality axis!), a genuinely text region with low-quality scan noise may get misclassified as "figure" just because OCR failed on it, which then means it's silently dropped from your knowledge base entirely. That's a nasty failure mode for exactly the pages you're supposed to be showing robustness on.

It can't distinguish "table" or "heading" from "text" — everything Tesseract reads confidently collapses into one bucket. Your Region.kind contract supports 4 kinds; you're really only using 2.

It's ~2x OCR cost per page (unless you cache), and it's a heuristic dressed up as a "smarter" method, not actually a layout model — a grader who looks closely will see it's OCR-confidence-as-a-proxy, not real layout understanding.

A trained layout detector (Detectron2+PubLayNet, or something lighter like doclayout-yolo / PP-StructureV2) is genuinely the better approach because it separates "where is text" from "can I read it" — which is the whole point of having Stage 2 and Stage 3 as separate pipeline stages in the first place. It also gets you real 4-way region typing, and it's a stronger, more literature-grounded story for Section 3's reproduced-pretrained-method requirement (layout detection is a real, citable ML task; OCR-confidence-as-a-classifier isn't something you'd find in a paper).

Given your actual constraints, here's what I'd do:

Try doclayout-yolo or a PP-StructureV2 checkpoint via paddleocr's layout module first, before giving up on trained detectors entirely. Both are much lighter than Detectron2 — pure pip install, no build toolchain, no Dropbox-config fetch (they pull from HuggingFace Hub, which is far more reliable), and they run fine on CPU or your RTX GPU on Windows without the macOS/Detectron2 pain we hit earlier. That solves the original blocker without the compromises of the OCR-heuristic route.

If that also turns out to be more setup fighting than it's worth this week, use the OCR-signal method as your documented fallback — but say so explicitly in design_choices.md: "tried Detectron2 (blocked by platform/dependency issues), tried a lightweight YOLO-based layout model as X, fell back to OCR-confidence heuristic because Y" — that's a much stronger design story than silently shipping the heuristic as if it were the first choice.

Want me to write the doclayout-yolo (or PaddleOCR layout) version of detect() so you can try that route before committing to the fallback?

Here's the doclayout-yolo implementation. It's pip-installable (no conda/build toolchain needed), pulls its checkpoint from Hugging Face Hub (reliable, unlike the Dropbox issue you hit with Detectron2), and runs on CPU or your RTX GPU.

pip install doclayout-yolo huggingface_hub

# vision/layout.py
from __future__ import annotations
import cv2
from doclayout_yolo import YOLOv10
from huggingface_hub import hf_hub_download
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

# DocStructBench's 10 fine-grained classes, collapsed onto your 4-way Region.kind
_ID_TO_NAME = {
    0: "title", 1: "plain text", 2: "abandon", 3: "figure", 4: "figure_caption",
    5: "table", 6: "table_caption", 7: "table_footnote", 8: "isolate_formula", 9: "formula_caption",
}
_NAME_TO_KIND = {
    "title": "heading",
    "plain text": "text",
    "abandon": "text",          # headers/footers/page numbers/marginalia -> still text, just low-value
    "figure": "figure",
    "figure_caption": "text",   # captions are readable prose, not the image itself
    "table": "table",
    "table_caption": "text",
    "table_footnote": "text",
    "isolate_formula": "figure",  # not real text for a Bangla-OCR reader; treat like a figure
    "formula_caption": "text",
}

_MODEL = None

def _get_model():
    global _MODEL
    if _MODEL is None:
        weights_path = hf_hub_download(
            repo_id="juliozhao/DocLayout-YOLO-DocStructBench",
            filename="doclayout_yolo_docstructbench_imgsz1024.pt",
        )
        _MODEL = YOLOv10(weights_path)
    return _MODEL

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    model = _get_model()
    conf_thr = cfg["layout"].get("score_thr", 0.25)
    device = cfg.get("device", "cpu")

    regions: list[Region] = []
    for page in pages:
        det = model.predict(page.image_path, imgsz=1024, conf=conf_thr, device=device)[0]
        boxes = det.boxes.xyxy.cpu().numpy()
        classes = det.boxes.cls.cpu().numpy()

        for box, cls_id in zip(boxes, classes):
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, box)
            name = _ID_TO_NAME.get(int(cls_id), "plain text")
            kind = _NAME_TO_KIND.get(name, "text")
            regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=(x1, y1, x2, y2), kind=kind))

    return regions

Notes specific to your case:

device: pull it from cfg["device"] (your config.yaml already has device: cuda) — set to "cpu" if you're testing on the Mac, "cuda:0" on your Windows/RTX box.

This model is trained on academic/textbook/financial documents, not 1930s–40s Bangla agricultural manuals — it's not fine-tuned to your domain, so expect it to be decent but not perfect on your old degraded scans, especially distinguishing hand-drawn botanical diagrams from photographic halftones (both count as "figure" for you anyway, so that's fine) versus occasionally misreading a heavily degraded text block as "abandon"/margin noise. Still — run visualize_regions() (your existing debug-drawing function works unchanged, it doesn't care which detector produced the boxes) on 5–6 pages including your worst scans before trusting it on all 749.

imgsz=1024 is the checkpoint's trained resolution — don't change it casually, since detection quality can degrade at other sizes (the GitHub issue thread I found even discusses tuning imgsz per document type). If your scans are notably larger/smaller, resizing the input via model.predict(..., imgsz=1024) handles the scaling internally, so you don't need to pre-resize yourself.

This now genuinely gives you the "≥1 reproduced pretrained method" credit for layout, on top of your Tesseract-ben reproduction for OCR — two, if you want to mention both in Section 3, though one is enough to satisfy the requirement.

Run it on your 4 sample pages first and compare against your visualize_regions() output from the OpenCV version — that side-by-side is good evidence to drop straight into design_choices.md's Stage 2 row ("compared classical heuristic vs. pretrained YOLO layout model; chose the latter for X, Y").

Done

Real progress — headings and multi-paragraph text are now separating correctly, which was the whole point. Two remaining problems stand out across your samples, and they're a different category of bug than before:

1. Missed detections (false negatives), not misclassifications. In Image 1, the page title bar ("ভারতের কৃষি-ব্যবস্থার পরিচয়") has no box at all now — it wasn't just mislabeled, it was never detected. Same in Image 4: the bottom caption ("ফটো নং ৭...গ্রহণ করিয়া থাকেন।") is completely unboxed. This is actually worse for your robust-and-reliable NFR than a wrong label — a misclassified region still gets some processing downstream; an undetected region means that text silently never makes it into your chunks/index at all, and you'd never know unless you specifically checked page coverage.

2. Overlapping/duplicate boxes on adjacent paragraphs (visible in Image 2, where two paragraph boxes touch/slightly overlap at the boundary) — will produce duplicate or split chunks for the same text.

Both likely stem from conf=0.25 combined with imgsz=1024 — DocLayout-YOLO's pretrained checkpoint was trained on modern academic/textbook scans, and thin single-line captions/titles on your degraded, lower-contrast historical pages just aren't scoring above threshold at that resolution. Here's a fix that addresses all three things at once: recall (lower conf), scale sensitivity (larger imgsz), duplicate boxes (merge pass), and — the important one for your NFR — a coverage guarantee so nothing silently vanishes.

# vision/layout.py
from __future__ import annotations
import cv2
import numpy as np
from doclayout_yolo import YOLOv10
from huggingface_hub import hf_hub_download
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

_ID_TO_NAME = {
    0: "title", 1: "plain text", 2: "abandon", 3: "figure", 4: "figure_caption",
    5: "table", 6: "table_caption", 7: "table_footnote", 8: "isolate_formula", 9: "formula_caption",
}
_NAME_TO_KIND = {
    "title": "heading", "plain text": "text", "abandon": "text", "figure": "figure",
    "figure_caption": "text", "table": "table", "table_caption": "text",
    "table_footnote": "text", "isolate_formula": "figure", "formula_caption": "text",
}

_MODEL = None

def _get_model():
    global _MODEL
    if _MODEL is None:
        weights_path = hf_hub_download(
            repo_id="juliozhao/DocLayout-YOLO-DocStructBench",
            filename="doclayout_yolo_docstructbench_imgsz1024.pt",
        )
        _MODEL = YOLOv10(weights_path)
    return _MODEL

def _iou(a, b):
    ax1, ay1, ax2, ay2 = a; bx1, by1, bx2, by2 = b
    ix1, iy1 = max(ax1, bx1), max(ay1, by1)
    ix2, iy2 = min(ax2, bx2), min(ay2, by2)
    iw, ih = max(0, ix2 - ix1), max(0, iy2 - iy1)
    inter = iw * ih
    area_a = (ax2 - ax1) * (ay2 - ay1)
    area_b = (bx2 - bx1) * (by2 - by1)
    union = area_a + area_b - inter
    return inter / union if union > 0 else 0

def _merge_overlaps(boxes_kinds: list[tuple[tuple, str]], iou_thr: float = 0.3):
    """Merge same-kind boxes that overlap significantly (fixes duplicate/split paragraph boxes)."""
    merged = []
    used = [False] * len(boxes_kinds)
    for i, (box_i, kind_i) in enumerate(boxes_kinds):
        if used[i]:
            continue
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = box_i
        used[i] = True
        for j in range(i + 1, len(boxes_kinds)):
            if used[j]:
                continue
            box_j, kind_j = boxes_kinds[j]
            if kind_j == kind_i and _iou(box_i, box_j) > iou_thr:
                jx1, jy1, jx2, jy2 = box_j
                x1, y1, x2, y2 = min(x1, jx1), min(y1, jy1), max(x2, jx2), max(y2, jy2)
                used[j] = True
        merged.append(((x1, y1, x2, y2), kind_i))
    return merged

def _uncovered_regions(gray: np.ndarray, boxes: list[tuple]) -> list[tuple[int, int, int, int]]:
    """Find page area with real ink content that no detected box covers — the coverage safety net."""
    mask = np.zeros(gray.shape, dtype=np.uint8)
    for x1, y1, x2, y2 in boxes:
        mask[y1:y2, x1:x2] = 255
    _, ink = cv2.threshold(gray, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV | cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    missed_ink = cv2.bitwise_and(ink, cv2.bitwise_not(mask))
    kernel = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_RECT, (25, 15))
    missed_ink = cv2.dilate(missed_ink, kernel, iterations=2)
    contours, _ = cv2.findContours(missed_ink, cv2.RETR_EXTERNAL, cv2.CHAIN_APPROX_SIMPLE)
    out = []
    for c in contours:
        x, y, w, h = cv2.boundingRect(c)
        if w * h > 0.002 * gray.shape[0] * gray.shape[1]:  # skip stray noise specks
            out.append((x, y, x + w, y + h))
    return out

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    model = _get_model()
    conf_thr = cfg["layout"].get("score_thr", 0.15)   # lowered from 0.25 for recall on faint/small elements
    imgsz = cfg["layout"].get("imgsz", 1280)           # bumped from 1024 for small captions/titles
    device = cfg.get("device", "cpu")

    regions: list[Region] = []
    for page in pages:
        det = model.predict(page.image_path, imgsz=imgsz, conf=conf_thr, device=device)[0]
        raw_boxes = det.boxes.xyxy.cpu().numpy()
        classes = det.boxes.cls.cpu().numpy()

        boxes_kinds = []
        for box, cls_id in zip(raw_boxes, classes):
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, box)
            name = _ID_TO_NAME.get(int(cls_id), "plain text")
            boxes_kinds.append(((x1, y1, x2, y2), _NAME_TO_KIND.get(name, "text")))

        boxes_kinds = _merge_overlaps(boxes_kinds)

        # coverage safety net: anything with ink that no box touched gets swept in as "text"
        gray = cv2.imread(page.image_path, cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE)
        covered = [b for b, _ in boxes_kinds]
        for gap_box in _uncovered_regions(gray, covered):
            boxes_kinds.append((gap_box, "text"))

        for (x1, y1, x2, y2), kind in boxes_kinds:
            regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=(x1, y1, x2, y2), kind=kind))

    return regions

What changed and why it targets your exact failures:

conf=0.15 + imgsz=1280: catches faint/small elements like the title bar and short captions that were scoring below 0.25 at 1024px — degraded, low-contrast scans especially need this.

_merge_overlaps(): fixes Image 2's touching/duplicate paragraph boxes by merging same-kind boxes that overlap past an IoU threshold, instead of leaving two boxes claiming the same text.

_uncovered_regions(): this is the important one for your NFR story — it's a deterministic backstop, not another ML guess. After YOLO does its pass, it scans for any page area with real ink that no box touched, and force-adds it as "text" rather than letting it disappear. Worst case it mislabels a stray figure fragment as text (fixable downstream by ocr.transcribe() returning near-empty output for it); best case it catches exactly what happened in Images 1 and 4.

One thing worth adding to notebooks/kb_demo.ipynb or reports/eval_report.md since you're targeting robust-and-reliable: log page ink-coverage % (covered-by-boxes area ÷ total ink area) per page as a metric. It gives you an honest, checkable number for "how much of the page did we actually capture" — good evidence for Section 5's "worst failure we saw" cell, and a concrete way to show the coverage net is doing its job rather than just asserting it.

Try this on your 4 sample pages first — specifically check whether the title bar and bottom caption now get boxed, and whether Image 2's paragraph boundary is a single clean box instead of two overlapping ones.

Done

Good — this batch surfaces genuinely different bugs than before, and they're in the code I gave you last round, so let's fix those directly. Three separate things went wrong:

1. The coverage-net defaults everything to "text" — that's a bug I left in. Image 3's bottom photo is a real figure that YOLO's pretrained model completely missed (badly degraded halftone, no text on it at all), so my _uncovered_regions() caught it correctly as "missing" — but then I hardcoded kind="text" for anything it finds, so it got labeled backwards. That's the most damaging bug here: a whole-page photo now flows into OCR as if it were readable prose.

2. The coverage-net's dilation is too aggressive and bridges across unrelated content. In Image 4, look at the big green box spanning from the title down through both flower diagrams — that's the fallback net picking up scattered pixels (the "Stig.", "St.", "c", "O" annotation labels that sit just outside each figure's tight YOLO bbox), then dilating them so much they chain together into one giant blob covering the heading and both figures. Same mechanism explains the stray tiny box in Image 3's top-right corner — a scratch/artifact on the scan getting swept in.

3. _merge_overlaps shouldn't apply to figures at all. In Image 4, the two separate flower diagrams got fused into a single red box. That function was written to fix duplicate/split text paragraphs — merging two genuinely distinct figures just because their boxes happen to be close together is a different bug with the same symptom (overlapping boxes), and needs a different fix: don't merge figures, only merge text/heading/table.

# vision/layout.py
from __future__ import annotations
import cv2
import numpy as np
import pytesseract
from pytesseract import Output
from doclayout_yolo import YOLOv10
from huggingface_hub import hf_hub_download
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

_ID_TO_NAME = {
    0: "title", 1: "plain text", 2: "abandon", 3: "figure", 4: "figure_caption",
    5: "table", 6: "table_caption", 7: "table_footnote", 8: "isolate_formula", 9: "formula_caption",
}
_NAME_TO_KIND = {
    "title": "heading", "plain text": "text", "abandon": "text", "figure": "figure",
    "figure_caption": "text", "table": "table", "table_caption": "text",
    "table_footnote": "text", "isolate_formula": "figure", "formula_caption": "text",
}
_MERGE_KINDS = {"text", "heading", "table"}  # never merge "figure" — distinct figures must stay separate

_MODEL = None

def _get_model():
    global _MODEL
    if _MODEL is None:
        weights_path = hf_hub_download(
            repo_id="juliozhao/DocLayout-YOLO-DocStructBench",
            filename="doclayout_yolo_docstructbench_imgsz1024.pt",
        )
        _MODEL = YOLOv10(weights_path)
    return _MODEL

def _iou(a, b):
    ax1, ay1, ax2, ay2 = a; bx1, by1, bx2, by2 = b
    ix1, iy1 = max(ax1, bx1), max(ay1, by1)
    ix2, iy2 = min(ax2, bx2), min(ay2, by2)
    iw, ih = max(0, ix2 - ix1), max(0, iy2 - iy1)
    inter = iw * ih
    area_a = (ax2 - ax1) * (ay2 - ay1)
    area_b = (bx2 - bx1) * (by2 - by1)
    union = area_a + area_b - inter
    return inter / union if union > 0 else 0

def _merge_overlaps(boxes_kinds: list[tuple[tuple, str]], iou_thr: float = 0.3):
    merged = []
    used = [False] * len(boxes_kinds)
    for i, (box_i, kind_i) in enumerate(boxes_kinds):
        if used[i]:
            continue
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = box_i
        used[i] = True
        if kind_i in _MERGE_KINDS:  # only merge text-like boxes; figures stay as-is
            for j in range(i + 1, len(boxes_kinds)):
                if used[j]:
                    continue
                box_j, kind_j = boxes_kinds[j]
                if kind_j == kind_i and _iou(box_i, box_j) > iou_thr:
                    jx1, jy1, jx2, jy2 = box_j
                    x1, y1, x2, y2 = min(x1, jx1), min(y1, jy1), max(x2, jx2), max(y2, jy2)
                    used[j] = True
        merged.append(((x1, y1, x2, y2), kind_i))
    return merged

def _classify_gap(gray: np.ndarray, box: tuple[int, int, int, int], min_conf: float = 40.0, min_words: int = 3) -> str:
    """Decide what an UNDETECTED region actually is, instead of assuming 'text'."""
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = box
    crop = gray[y1:y2, x1:x2]
    try:
        data = pytesseract.image_to_data(crop, lang="ben", output_type=Output.DICT)
        confident_words = [w for w, c in zip(data["text"], data["conf"]) if w.strip() and float(c) >= min_conf]
        if len(confident_words) >= min_words:
            return "text"
    except Exception:
        pass
    return "figure"  # no readable words -> treat as figure, not text

def _uncovered_regions(
    gray: np.ndarray,
    boxes: list[tuple[int, int, int, int]],
    pad: int = 8,
    min_area_frac: float = 0.004,
    kernel_size: tuple[int, int] = (15, 9),
    iterations: int = 1,
) -> list[tuple[int, int, int, int]]:
    mask = np.zeros(gray.shape, dtype=np.uint8)
    h, w = gray.shape
    for x1, y1, x2, y2 in boxes:
        # pad each covered box outward so labels/arrows just outside a figure's tight
        # bbox aren't treated as separate "missed" content that then bridges elsewhere
        x1p, y1p = max(0, x1 - pad), max(0, y1 - pad)
        x2p, y2p = min(w, x2 + pad), min(h, y2 + pad)
        mask[y1p:y2p, x1p:x2p] = 255

    _, ink = cv2.threshold(gray, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV | cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    missed_ink = cv2.bitwise_and(ink, cv2.bitwise_not(mask))
    kernel = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_RECT, kernel_size)   # smaller + fewer iterations
    missed_ink = cv2.dilate(missed_ink, kernel, iterations=iterations)  # than before: less bridging

    contours, _ = cv2.findContours(missed_ink, cv2.RETR_EXTERNAL, cv2.CHAIN_APPROX_SIMPLE)
    out = []
    for c in contours:
        x, y, cw, ch = cv2.boundingRect(c)
        if cw * ch > min_area_frac * h * w and min(cw, ch) > 10:  # drop scratches/speckle noise
            out.append((x, y, x + cw, y + ch))
    return out

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    model = _get_model()
    conf_thr = cfg["layout"].get("score_thr", 0.15)
    imgsz = cfg["layout"].get("imgsz", 1280)
    device = cfg.get("device", "cpu")

    regions: list[Region] = []
    for page in pages:
        det = model.predict(page.image_path, imgsz=imgsz, conf=conf_thr, device=device)[0]
        raw_boxes = det.boxes.xyxy.cpu().numpy()
        classes = det.boxes.cls.cpu().numpy()

        boxes_kinds = []
        for box, cls_id in zip(raw_boxes, classes):
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, box)
            name = _ID_TO_NAME.get(int(cls_id), "plain text")
            boxes_kinds.append(((x1, y1, x2, y2), _NAME_TO_KIND.get(name, "text")))

        boxes_kinds = _merge_overlaps(boxes_kinds)

        gray = cv2.imread(page.image_path, cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE)
        covered = [b for b, _ in boxes_kinds]
        for gap_box in _uncovered_regions(gray, covered):
            kind = _classify_gap(gray, gap_box)   # actually decide text-vs-figure now
            boxes_kinds.append((gap_box, kind))

        for (x1, y1, x2, y2), kind in boxes_kinds:
            regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=(x1, y1, x2, y2), kind=kind))

    return regions

What each change targets:

_classify_gap() reuses the OCR-confidence check from earlier — now the fallback net makes an actual decision instead of assuming "text", so Image 3's missed photo should land as "figure".

pad=8 on covered boxes before computing the missed-ink mask stops annotation labels/arrow lines sitting just outside a figure's YOLO bbox from being treated as "new" uncovered content — this is what should stop the title→figure bridging in Image 4.

Smaller kernel (15,9) + iterations=1 (down from (25,15)/2) reduces how far dilation can chain separate blobs together, and min(cw,ch) > 10 filters out thin scratch marks like Image 3's stray corner box.

_merge_overlaps now only touches {text, heading, table} — figures are left exactly as YOLO/coverage-net produced them, so two adjacent-but-distinct diagrams won't get fused.

One thing I noticed but didn't touch: page-number boxes are inconsistent (Image 1's "২" isn't boxed, Image 2's "৩" is). Low priority — page furniture, not real content — but if you want it consistent, it's a min_area_frac tuning question, and worth a one-line mention in design_choices.md either way ("small page-number regions inconsistently captured; low priority, doesn't affect answer quality").

Re-run on these same 4 pages and check specifically: does Image 3's bottom photo turn red, does Image 4's title box shrink back to just the title, and do the two flower diagrams stay as two separate red boxes.

Done

Honestly — probably not, and here's the reasoning rather than just an opinion:

What's actually been going wrong isn't model quality. Every bug across our last two rounds (missed title/caption, bridging boxes, fused figures) came from my postprocessing code — the coverage-net dilation, the merge logic, the hardcoded fallback kind — not from DocLayout-YOLO's raw detections being bad. The core YOLO predictions have actually been reasonable each time; it's the patches around them that kept misbehaving. Swapping the base model won't fix bugs that live in the merge/coverage layer — you'd just be debugging the same category of issue against a different backbone.

No pretrained layout model — DocLayout-YOLO, PP-DocLayout, LayoutLMv3, whatever — was trained on 1930s-era degraded Bangla agricultural scans. They're all trained on modern academic papers, textbooks, magazines, financial reports. So you'll hit some rate of missed/wrong detections on your specific corpus no matter which one you pick, and you'll always need some kind of fallback/coverage layer — which means you can't fully escape the class of bug you've been fixing regardless of model choice.

PP-DocLayout (PaddleOCR) is the other realistic option, and it is somewhat stronger on paper — its plus-L variant specifically claims better performance on "complex documents... handwritten documents." But practically, for your setup: paddlepaddle-gpu officially wants WSL or Docker on Windows (not native pip), which reintroduces exactly the kind of environment friction that made us drop Detectron2 in the first place. Not worth trading a working pipeline for that risk this close to the A2 deadline.

My actual recommendation: stay on DocLayout-YOLO, and spend remaining time tightening the postprocessing instead — specifically, sanity-check the current version against a wider sample (15–20 pages, not 4) so you're tuning against real variance rather than reacting to one page's bug at a time. That's a better use of your hours than a model swap that trades one set of edge cases for another.

If you do want to try PP-DocLayout later as an A3/A4 stretch comparison (or just to see the numbers side by side for configs/design_choices.md's "options compared" column), it's a legitimate thing to note even without switching — "considered PP-DocLayout, deferred due to Windows/Paddle packaging overhead" is a fine, honest line in that table. Want me to help you build the wider-sample eyeball check instead — something that dumps visualize_regions() output for a random 20-page sample so you can spot real patterns rather than one-off failures?

Done

Good catch, and also — I should flag something first: the layout.py you just pasted is the version from two messages ago, before the padding/smaller-kernel/_classify_gap fixes. That's why Image 1 and Image 2 show the exact same bugs as before (the green box wrapping around the whole figure in Image 1, the stray sliver box top-right in Image 2, the missed bottom photo in Image 2 still defaulting to "text"). Worth swapping in the fixed version before worrying about anything else — right now you're debugging a version I already patched.

Now, your actual question: yes, you're right, and it is a real duplication — but only a small, cheap one, not a full re-run of your pipeline's OCR stage. Here's the distinction:

_classify_gap() runs pytesseract.image_to_data() only on the small leftover gap regions YOLO missed — typically a handful of small boxes per page, not the whole page.

ocr.transcribe() in Stage 3 runs OCR on every region (including those same gap regions) to actually get the transcribed text for your chunks.

So for most pages the overlap is small. But it's still wasted work, and worth fixing properly since you're optimizing for robust-and-reliable — cleaner design also just makes a better story in design_choices.md.

Best fix: cache the OCR result from the gap check and reuse it in Stage 3, instead of throwing it away and re-running Tesseract on the same crop later.

# vision/layout.py
_gap_ocr_cache: dict[tuple, str] = {}  # (page_id, bbox) -> transcribed text, filled during gap classification

def _classify_gap(gray: np.ndarray, page_id: str, box: tuple[int, int, int, int],
                   min_conf: float = 40.0, min_words: int = 3) -> str:
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = box
    crop = gray[y1:y2, x1:x2]
    try:
        data = pytesseract.image_to_data(crop, lang="ben", output_type=Output.DICT)
        words = [w for w, c in zip(data["text"], data["conf"]) if w.strip() and float(c) >= min_conf]
        if len(words) >= min_words:
            _gap_ocr_cache[(page_id, box)] = " ".join(words)  # cache it — Stage 3 can reuse this
            return "text"
    except Exception:
        pass
    return "figure"

Then in vision/ocr.py's transcribe(), check the cache before calling the reader again:

# vision/ocr.py
from .layout import _gap_ocr_cache

def transcribe(regions: list[Region], cfg: dict) -> list[Chunk]:
    reader = Reader(cfg)
    chunks = []
    for r in regions:
        if r.kind == "figure":
            continue
        cached = _gap_ocr_cache.get((r.page_id, r.bbox))
        text = cached if cached is not None else reader.transcribe_region(r)
        chunks.append(Chunk(id=..., doc_id=..., text=text, page_ids=[r.page_id]))
    return chunks

Trade-off worth knowing: image_to_data (used for classification) and however you're calling Tesseract in Reader.transcribe_region (probably image_to_string) can format text slightly differently (word-joined-by-spaces vs. line-structured). Sanity check a few cached vs. non-cached chunks side by side in kb_demo.ipynb before trusting it fully — if the quality differs noticeably, it's simpler and safer to just accept the small duplicate cost and drop the cache idea entirely. Given your corpus is only 749 pages and gap regions are a minority of total regions, that duplication is unlikely to be a real bottleneck — I'd only bother with the cache if you actually measure OCR being your slow stage.

Investigated caching logic and identified potential data loss issues.

Done

That's the caching change I just gave you — and it's my fault, not a mystery bug. The problem is in _classify_gap()'s reuse: pytesseract.image_to_data() returns individual words with no guaranteed reading order, and " ".join(words) after filtering by confidence throws away line breaks, word order, and punctuation, plus silently drops any word below min_conf=40. That's a much lower-quality transcript than Reader.transcribe_region() normally produces via image_to_string (or however you're calling Tesseract in Stage 3), which preserves line structure properly.

I flagged this risk at the end of my last message but should've led with it more clearly — sorry. Given your corpus is only 749 pages and gap regions are a minority of total regions, the OCR "double work" isn't actually a real bottleneck worth optimizing for. Simplest fix: drop the cache entirely and let Stage 3 re-run OCR properly on gap regions, same as every other region:

# vision/layout.py — remove _gap_ocr_cache and the caching write in _classify_gap;
# just classify, don't try to reuse the result:
def _classify_gap(gray: np.ndarray, box: tuple[int, int, int, int],
                   min_conf: float = 40.0, min_words: int = 3) -> str:
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = box
    crop = gray[y1:y2, x1:x2]
    try:
        data = pytesseract.image_to_data(crop, lang="ben", output_type=Output.DICT)
        words = [w for w, c in zip(data["text"], data["conf"]) if w.strip() and float(c) >= min_conf]
        return "text" if len(words) >= min_words else "figure"
    except Exception:
        return "figure"

# vision/ocr.py — back to always calling the reader, no cache lookup
def transcribe(regions: list[Region], cfg: dict) -> list[Chunk]:
    reader = Reader(cfg)
    chunks = []
    for r in regions:
        if r.kind == "figure":
            continue
        text = reader.transcribe_region(r)
        chunks.append(Chunk(id=..., doc_id=..., text=text, page_ids=[r.page_id]))
    return chunks

Yes, this means gap regions get OCR'd twice (once cheaply for the yes/no text check, once properly for the real transcript) — but that's a minor efficiency cost, not a correctness one, and it's the safer trade given where you are in the timeline. Worth one honest line in design_choices.md: "layout gap-fallback re-runs OCR redundantly on a small subset of regions; accepted for simplicity given corpus size."

Also — remember the layout.py you showed me in your last message was the pre-padding version (before I fixed the coverage-net bridging and hardcoded-"text" bugs). Make sure you're running the version with pad=8, the smaller (15,9) kernel, and _classify_gap returning "figure" when it finds no text — otherwise you're still seeing those older bugs layered on top of this one. Want to paste your current full file so I can check it end-to-end in one pass instead of patching piece by piece?

0: "title", 1: "plain text", 2: "abandon", 3: "figure", 4: "figure_caption",

"title": "heading",
"plain text": "text",
"abandon": "text",          # headers/footers/page numbers/marginalia -> still text, just low-value
"figure": "figure",
"figure_caption": "text",   # captions are readable prose, not the image itself
"table": "table",
"table_caption": "text",
"table_footnote": "text",
"isolate_formula": "figure",  # not real text for a Bangla-OCR reader; treat like a figure
"formula_caption": "text",
}

        weights_path = hf_hub_download(
            repo_id="juliozhao/DocLayout-YOLO-DocStructBench",
            filename="doclayout_yolo_docstructbench_imgsz1024.pt",
        )
        _MODEL = YOLOv10(weights_path)
return _MODEL

            kind = _NAME_TO_KIND.get(name, "text")
            regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=(x1, y1, x2, y2), kind=kind))

Done

Good instinct dropping _merge_overlaps() — that was the thing fusing your two flower diagrams together earlier, and going back to raw YOLO output was the right call once you'd actually eyeballed 20 pages and confirmed it's clean. Don't re-add that merge logic unless you see real duplicate-box evidence on a wider sample; it caused more damage than it fixed.

What you're describing now — text existing on the page but landing in zero regions — is exactly the gap I originally tried to patch with _uncovered_regions(), just implemented more carefully this time given what broke last time. Since you've confirmed the base detector is solid, add only the coverage net, nothing else:

# vision/layout.py — add these, keep everything else exactly as you have it

import pytesseract
from pytesseract import Output

def _classify_gap(gray: np.ndarray, box: tuple[int, int, int, int],
                   min_conf: float = 40.0, min_words: int = 3) -> str:
    """Undetected region: is it real readable text, or should it stay untouched (figure)?"""
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = box
    crop = gray[y1:y2, x1:x2]
    try:
        data = pytesseract.image_to_data(crop, lang="ben", output_type=Output.DICT)
        words = [w for w, c in zip(data["text"], data["conf"]) if w.strip() and float(c) >= min_conf]
        return "text" if len(words) >= min_words else "figure"
    except Exception:
        return "figure"

def _uncovered_regions(
    gray: np.ndarray,
    boxes: list[tuple[int, int, int, int]],
    pad: int = 8,             # expand existing boxes before checking gaps, so labels/arrows
                               # just outside a figure's bbox aren't treated as "missed" content
    min_area_frac: float = 0.004,
    kernel_size: tuple[int, int] = (15, 9),   # small kernel — avoid bridging unrelated blobs together
    iterations: int = 1,
) -> list[tuple[int, int, int, int]]:
    h, w = gray.shape
    mask = np.zeros(gray.shape, dtype=np.uint8)
    for x1, y1, x2, y2 in boxes:
        x1p, y1p = max(0, x1 - pad), max(0, y1 - pad)
        x2p, y2p = min(w, x2 + pad), min(h, y2 + pad)
        mask[y1p:y2p, x1p:x2p] = 255

    _, ink = cv2.threshold(gray, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV | cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    missed_ink = cv2.bitwise_and(ink, cv2.bitwise_not(mask))
    kernel = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_RECT, kernel_size)
    missed_ink = cv2.dilate(missed_ink, kernel, iterations=iterations)

    contours, _ = cv2.findContours(missed_ink, cv2.RETR_EXTERNAL, cv2.CHAIN_APPROX_SIMPLE)
    out = []
    for c in contours:
        x, y, cw, ch = cv2.boundingRect(c)
        if cw * ch > min_area_frac * h * w and min(cw, ch) > 10:  # drop scratches/speckle
            out.append((x, y, x + cw, y + ch))
    return out

And the only change to detect():

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    model = _get_model()
    conf_thr = cfg["layout"].get("score_thr", 0.25)
    device = cfg.get("device", "cpu")

    regions: list[Region] = []
    for page in pages:
        det = model.predict(page.image_path, imgsz=1024, conf=conf_thr, device=device)[0]
        boxes = det.boxes.xyxy.cpu().numpy()
        classes = det.boxes.cls.cpu().numpy()

        page_regions = []
        for box, cls_id in zip(boxes, classes):
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, box)
            name = _ID_TO_NAME.get(int(cls_id), "plain text")
            kind = _NAME_TO_KIND.get(name, "text")
            page_regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=(x1, y1, x2, y2), kind=kind))

        gray = cv2.imread(page.image_path, cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE)
        covered = [r.bbox for r in page_regions]
        for gap_box in _uncovered_regions(gray, covered):
            kind = _classify_gap(gray, gap_box)
            page_regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=gap_box, kind=kind))

        regions.extend(page_regions)

    visualize_regions(pages, regions, output_dir="data/debug_layout")
    return regions

No merging, no caching, no hardcoded "text" default — just: keep what already works, and only add boxes for ink YOLO didn't touch at all, classified honestly by whether Tesseract can actually read them. This is deliberately the minimal version, since you've now validated the base detector is trustworthy on 20 pages — the coverage net should be a small correction, not something that reshapes the output.

Re-run on your same 20-page sample and specifically check: did the previously-missing text now get boxed, and — just as important — did anything that was already working correctly stay the same? If you spot the net accidentally grabbing figure-annotation labels (arrows/letters like "Stig.", "O", "c" from Image 1 a few messages back) as new text boxes, that's the pad value to bump up, not a sign to add merging back.

Here's the consolidated final version — your validated DocLayout-YOLO detector as the primary method, plus only the coverage-net addition (no merging, no caching), with docstrings tying each piece back to the course's conceptual framing (C5/C17 for the primary detector, a noted-but-not-implemented hook for C16 since layout-as-graph is optional, not required for A2):

# vision/layout.py
"""Stage 2 — layout detection / segmentation.

Detects text/heading/figure/table regions on each page so that Stage 3 (OCR)
runs on the right pieces, and skips regions (figures) that shouldn't be OCR'd.

Method (C5, C17): DocLayout-YOLO — a CNN detection backbone (C5 lineage) doing
dense region proposal + classification (C17) over 10 fine-grained document
classes, pretrained on DocStructBench and reused here via `predict()` for the
"≥1 reproduced pretrained method" requirement (Section 3).

Not implemented (C16, optional): reading-order / layout-as-graph modeling
(treating regions as graph nodes with a GNN or the pointer-network approach
PP-DocLayoutV2 uses) is the one place structured, permutation-aware modeling
is native to this stage. Region order here is left as YOLO's raw detection
order; `index/chunk.py` should not assume top-to-bottom reading order without
a stable sort (e.g. by (y1, x1)) before splitting.

Fallback net: DocLayout-YOLO is trained on modern academic/textbook/report
scans, not 1930s-40s degraded Bangla agricultural manuals, so recall on faint
or unusual elements (thin captions, worn titles) is imperfect. `_uncovered_regions`
sweeps up any leftover ink no detected box touched, and `_classify_gap` decides
text-vs-figure for that leftover ink using OCR confidence rather than assuming
a default kind — this is the E1-speciality (degraded scans) mitigation for
this stage; see design_choices.md, Stage 2 row, for the comparison against the
classical OpenCV heuristic and Detectron2/PubLayNet that were tried first.
"""
from __future__ import annotations
import os
import cv2
import numpy as np
import pytesseract
from pytesseract import Output
from doclayout_yolo import YOLOv10
from huggingface_hub import hf_hub_download
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

_COLOR_MAP = {
    "text": (0, 255, 0),
    "figure": (0, 0, 255),
    "table": (255, 0, 0),
    "heading": (0, 255, 255),
}

# DocStructBench's 10 fine-grained classes, collapsed onto the 4-way Region.kind
# used throughout the rest of the pipeline (contracts.py).
_ID_TO_NAME = {
    0: "title", 1: "plain text", 2: "abandon", 3: "figure", 4: "figure_caption",
    5: "table", 6: "table_caption", 7: "table_footnote", 8: "isolate_formula", 9: "formula_caption",
}
_NAME_TO_KIND = {
    "title": "heading",
    "plain text": "text",
    "abandon": "text",            # headers/footers/page numbers/marginalia -> still readable text
    "figure": "figure",
    "figure_caption": "text",     # captions are prose, not the image itself
    "table": "table",
    "table_caption": "text",
    "table_footnote": "text",
    "isolate_formula": "figure",  # not meaningful text for a Bangla-OCR reader
    "formula_caption": "text",
}

_MODEL = None

def _get_model() -> YOLOv10:
    global _MODEL
    if _MODEL is None:
        weights_path = hf_hub_download(
            repo_id="juliozhao/DocLayout-YOLO-DocStructBench",
            filename="doclayout_yolo_docstructbench_imgsz1024.pt",
        )
        _MODEL = YOLOv10(weights_path)
    return _MODEL

def visualize_regions(pages: list[Page], regions: list[Region], output_dir: str = "data/debug_layout") -> None:
    """Saves page images with color-coded bounding boxes and labels for debugging.

    Not part of the pipeline contract — a development aid. Safe to call after
    detect() during A2 build-out; drop the call (or gate it behind a debug flag
    in cfg) once the stage is finalized so full 749-page runs aren't paying for
    debug image writes.
    """
    os.makedirs(output_dir, exist_ok=True)
    regions_by_page: dict[str, list[Region]] = {}
    for r in regions:
        regions_by_page.setdefault(r.page_id, []).append(r)

    for page in pages:
        img = cv2.imread(page.image_path)
        if img is None:
            continue
        for r in regions_by_page.get(page.id, []):
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = r.bbox
            color = _COLOR_MAP.get(r.kind, (255, 255, 255))
            cv2.rectangle(img, (x1, y1), (x2, y2), color, thickness=2)
            cv2.putText(img, r.kind, (x1, max(y1 - 5, 15)),
                        cv2.FONT_HERSHEY_SIMPLEX, 0.6, color, 2, cv2.LINE_AA)
        cv2.imwrite(os.path.join(output_dir, f"debug_{page.id}.png"), img)

def _classify_gap(gray: np.ndarray, box: tuple[int, int, int, int],
                   min_conf: float = 40.0, min_words: int = 3) -> str:
    """Decide what an UNDETECTED region actually is, rather than assuming a default kind."""
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = box
    crop = gray[y1:y2, x1:x2]
    try:
        data = pytesseract.image_to_data(crop, lang="ben", output_type=Output.DICT)
        words = [w for w, c in zip(data["text"], data["conf"]) if w.strip() and float(c) >= min_conf]
        return "text" if len(words) >= min_words else "figure"
    except Exception:
        return "figure"

def _uncovered_regions(
    gray: np.ndarray,
    boxes: list[tuple[int, int, int, int]],
    pad: int = 8,
    min_area_frac: float = 0.004,
    kernel_size: tuple[int, int] = (15, 9),
    iterations: int = 1,
) -> list[tuple[int, int, int, int]]:
    """Find page ink not covered by any detected box — the coverage safety net.

    `pad` expands each existing box before computing the missed-ink mask, so
    figure annotation labels/arrows sitting just outside a tight YOLO bbox
    aren't picked up as separate "new" content. Kernel/iterations are kept
    small deliberately to avoid bridging unrelated blobs into one giant box.
    """
    h, w = gray.shape
    mask = np.zeros(gray.shape, dtype=np.uint8)
    for x1, y1, x2, y2 in boxes:
        x1p, y1p = max(0, x1 - pad), max(0, y1 - pad)
        x2p, y2p = min(w, x2 + pad), min(h, y2 + pad)
        mask[y1p:y2p, x1p:x2p] = 255

    _, ink = cv2.threshold(gray, 0, 255, cv2.THRESH_BINARY_INV | cv2.THRESH_OTSU)
    missed_ink = cv2.bitwise_and(ink, cv2.bitwise_not(mask))
    kernel = cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_RECT, kernel_size)
    missed_ink = cv2.dilate(missed_ink, kernel, iterations=iterations)

    contours, _ = cv2.findContours(missed_ink, cv2.RETR_EXTERNAL, cv2.CHAIN_APPROX_SIMPLE)
    out = []
    for c in contours:
        x, y, cw, ch = cv2.boundingRect(c)
        if cw * ch > min_area_frac * h * w and min(cw, ch) > 10:  # drop scratches/speckle noise
            out.append((x, y, x + cw, y + ch))
    return out

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    model = _get_model()
    conf_thr = cfg["layout"].get("score_thr", 0.25)
    device = cfg.get("device", "cpu")
    debug = cfg["layout"].get("debug_visualize", False)

    regions: list[Region] = []
    for page in pages:
        det = model.predict(page.image_path, imgsz=1024, conf=conf_thr, device=device)[0]
        boxes = det.boxes.xyxy.cpu().numpy()
        classes = det.boxes.cls.cpu().numpy()

        page_regions: list[Region] = []
        for box, cls_id in zip(boxes, classes):
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, box)
            name = _ID_TO_NAME.get(int(cls_id), "plain text")
            kind = _NAME_TO_KIND.get(name, "text")
            page_regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=(x1, y1, x2, y2), kind=kind))

        gray = cv2.imread(page.image_path, cv2.IMREAD_GRAYSCALE)
        covered = [r.bbox for r in page_regions]
        for gap_box in _uncovered_regions(gray, covered):
            kind = _classify_gap(gray, gap_box)
            page_regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=gap_box, kind=kind))

        regions.extend(page_regions)

    if debug:
        visualize_regions(pages, regions)
    return regions

Two things worth doing before you consider this stage closed:

Gate visualize_regions() behind cfg["layout"]["debug_visualize"] (added above) instead of always writing debug PNGs — on a full 749-page run that's 749 extra image writes you don't need once you've validated the output, and run_ingest.py's stage-2 print (regions detected: ...) already gives you a numeric sanity check without it.

design_choices.md's Stage 2 row should now say something like: tried Detectron2/PubLayNet (blocked by Windows/macOS packaging), tried a classical OpenCV heuristic (failed on Bangla matra-joined connected components), settled on DocLayout-YOLO + an OCR-confidence coverage net for missed regions; validated visually on 20 sampled pages. That's a genuinely strong, honest design narrative for Section 3 — it shows iteration, not just a first guess that happened to work.

8/13/2026 13:57:50

Diagnosed detection failures and weighed conservative tuning options.

Diagnosed detection failures and weighed conservative tuning options.

Done

Useful to see this across 8 pages — the pattern is clearer now than it was on 4. All the problems here are recall misses (real content, zero box), not misclassifications:

Image 1: the subtitle "উদ্ভিদের গঠন ( Plant Forms )" right under the heading has no box at all.

Image 2: the table's own header row ("ব্যবহার" / "উদ্ভিদের নাম") and the entire left column of row labels ("খাদ্য", "শর্করা", "মশলা") are undetected — only the right-column values got boxed, as scattered "text", not even recognized as one "table".

Image 5: the whole caption block under the two figures (the "চিত্র নং ৫। বীজের গঠন..." description with sub-labels A/B/C) is completely unboxed.

Image 6: the formula block (বিশুদ্ধতার শতকরা হার × ... = ৯২) has no box, and three short one-line items get fragmented into separate tiny boxes instead of reading as one block — not wrong exactly, just noisy.

Images 3/4/7/8 are actually clean — worth noting, since it tells us the failures cluster around specific content types (formulas, table headers/labels, multi-line captions with mixed Bangla+English+numbering), not randomly across all pages.

Given the coverage-net attempts kept causing regressions, I'd deliberately not reach for custom heuristics again as the first move. Two changes, in order of risk, cheapest first:

1. Lower conf and raise imgsz — no new code, just tuning. All four misses above are subtle/small elements (short subtitle, table headers, numeric formula, caption text) — exactly what a confidence threshold that's too high silently drops. At conf=0.25, imgsz=1024 you're likely losing real detections below that score. Try:

conf_thr = cfg["layout"].get("score_thr", 0.10)   # down from 0.25
det = model.predict(page.image_path, imgsz=1280, conf=conf_thr, device=device)[0]  # up from 1024

This is much lower-risk than the coverage-net code, because it doesn't add any new logic that can misbehave — it just asks the same validated model to report more of what it already sees. Re-run on exactly these 8 pages and check whether the subtitle, formula, and table headers now appear. You may pick up a few false positives at this lower threshold (stray marks flagged as low-confidence boxes) — that's an acceptable trade to inspect and adjust, not a structural bug.

2. If lowering conf doesn't recover the table-header/caption misses, that's a genuine detector blind spot on this content type (visually sparse, no grid lines, mixed script/numbering) — not something more threshold-tuning will fix. At that point the honest, lowest-risk move is not another custom merge/bridge heuristic (which is what kept regressing), but the narrow coverage-net from two messages ago — the one with pad=8, small (15,9) kernel, and OCR-based gap classification — applied only to this specific pattern. Before trusting it broadly again, test it in isolation on just these 8 pages and diff against the current output: confirm it adds boxes for the subtitle/formula/table-labels without touching Image 3/4/7/8, which are already correct. If it changes anything on those clean pages, that's your signal to stop and not merge it in yet.

Diagnosed overlapping detections and engineered deduplication strategy.

Diagnosed overlapping detections and engineered deduplication strategy.

Done

Good diagnosis instinct, but before jumping to a custom position-based classifier — look closely at Image 2's top box again: "পঞ্চম অধ্যায়" has two overlapping boxes, one drawn in green (labeled "heading") and one right under it in near-black (also labeled "heading"). That's not actually a heading-vs-text disagreement — it's the same physical box detected twice by YOLO, both times classified as heading, just at slightly different coordinates/confidence. This is the real mechanism behind what looks like inconsistent classification: when you drop conf to 0.085 without deduplication, the model naturally emits multiple near-duplicate boxes for the same object (common at low thresholds — you're now seeing detections that would normally get suppressed). Since Ultralytics' NMS is typically class-aware, two overlapping boxes with different predicted classes for the same region won't get merged even though they're clearly the same object.

So the actual fix isn't a coordinate-based heading/text classifier — it's adding an explicit, class-agnostic NMS/dedup pass, which addresses both symptoms you're seeing (duplicate boxes, and the same region getting two different kind labels) with one well-understood mechanism, rather than a bespoke heuristic that's likely to misfire the way the earlier merge/coverage-net attempts did.

# vision/layout.py

def _iou(a, b):
    ax1, ay1, ax2, ay2 = a; bx1, by1, bx2, by2 = b
    ix1, iy1 = max(ax1, bx1), max(ay1, by1)
    ix2, iy2 = min(ax2, bx2), min(ay2, by2)
    iw, ih = max(0, ix2 - ix1), max(0, iy2 - iy1)
    inter = iw * ih
    area_a = (ax2 - ax1) * (ay2 - ay1)
    area_b = (bx2 - bx1) * (by2 - by1)
    union = area_a + area_b - inter
    return inter / union if union > 0 else 0

def _dedup_boxes(
    detections: list[tuple[tuple[int, int, int, int], str, float]],  # (bbox, kind, confidence)
    iou_thr: float = 0.5,
) -> list[tuple[tuple[int, int, int, int], str]]:
    """Class-agnostic dedup: when two boxes of ANY kind overlap heavily, they're almost
    certainly the same physical region detected twice — keep only the higher-confidence one.
    This is what class-aware NMS inside the model doesn't catch."""
    detections = sorted(detections, key=lambda d: d[2], reverse=True)  # highest confidence first
    kept: list[tuple[tuple[int, int, int, int], str, float]] = []
    for box, kind, conf in detections:
        if all(_iou(box, kb) < iou_thr for kb, _, _ in kept):
            kept.append((box, kind, conf))
    return [(box, kind) for box, kind, _ in kept]

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    model = _get_model()
    conf_thr = cfg["layout"].get("score_thr", 0.085)
    imgsz = cfg["layout"].get("imgsz", 1184)
    device = cfg.get("device", "cpu")
    dedup_iou = cfg["layout"].get("dedup_iou_thr", 0.5)

    regions: list[Region] = []
    for page in pages:
        det = model.predict(page.image_path, imgsz=imgsz, conf=conf_thr, device=device)[0]
        boxes = det.boxes.xyxy.cpu().numpy()
        classes = det.boxes.cls.cpu().numpy()
        confs = det.boxes.conf.cpu().numpy()   # need this now — kept alongside kind for dedup

        raw = []
        for box, cls_id, conf in zip(boxes, classes, confs):
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, box)
            name = _ID_TO_NAME.get(int(cls_id), "plain text")
            kind = _NAME_TO_KIND.get(name, "text")
            raw.append(((x1, y1, x2, y2), kind, float(conf)))

        deduped = _dedup_boxes(raw, iou_thr=dedup_iou)
        for (x1, y1, x2, y2), kind in deduped:
            regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=(x1, y1, x2, y2), kind=kind))

    visualize_regions(pages, regions)
    return regions

Formula regions (mathematical expressions, not readable Bangla prose) will now flow into ocr.transcribe() as if they were real text, which will likely produce OCR garbage in your chunks. If that was accidental, revert it back to "figure".

If it's still flip-flopping page to page after dedup, that's a genuinely different, smaller problem — a light tie-breaker specifically for that one recurring box (e.g., "if this near-identical bbox recurs across ≥N pages with inconsistent kind, force it to the majority-vote kind for that position") would be a reasonable, narrow addition.
