Evaluated header classification validity and devised containment-based deduplication strategy.

Evaluated header classification validity and devised containment-based deduplication strategy.

Done

Two different issues here, and they need different fixes.

On the header: this isn't actually a bug — I'd leave it. heading and text both flow into your chunks as real content (only figure gets skipped in ocr.transcribe()), so whether YOLO calls the repeating page title "heading" or "text" doesn't change what happens downstream — it just gets OCR'd and indexed either way. Running headers appearing as a small chunk on every page is normal for scanned books and won't break anything. If you specifically want to suppress them (so "ভারতের কৃষি-ব্যবস্থার পরিচয়" doesn't show up as 749 near-identical chunks diluting retrieval), that's a distinct, optional feature — detecting a bbox that recurs at nearly the same position across many pages and dropping/flagging it — but I'd treat that as a stretch goal, not something A2 needs. Not worth spending more time on right now given the deadline.

The overlap question is the real bug, and yes — you're right to worry about it. Look at Image 2's formula block: the big outer box ("বিশুদ্ধতার শতকরা হার × অঙ্কুরোদগমের শতকরা হার") fully contains three smaller boxes nested inside it ("১০০", "৯৮ × ৯৪ / ১০০", "৯২"). Since Stage 3 (ocr.transcribe) runs OCR on every region independently, this box structure means Tesseract reads the whole formula once for the outer box, and reads the same numbers again for each inner box — so yes, this will duplicate content in your chunks. This is a genuinely different failure mode than the earlier duplicate-detection issue: the boxes have very different areas (one big, several small), so their IoU is low even though one is fully sitting inside the other — my earlier _dedup_boxes() (which only checks IoU) won't catch this at all.

The fix is a containment check, not an IoU check — if a smaller box is almost entirely inside a larger box, drop the smaller one and trust the larger box's OCR to cover that content:

# vision/layout.py

def _containment_ratio(inner: tuple, outer: tuple) -> float:
    """What fraction of `inner`'s area sits inside `outer`."""
    ix1, iy1, ix2, iy2 = inner
    ox1, oy1, ox2, oy2 = outer
    x1, y1 = max(ix1, ox1), max(iy1, oy1)
    x2, y2 = min(ix2, ox2), min(iy2, oy2)
    inter = max(0, x2 - x1) * max(0, y2 - y1)
    inner_area = (ix2 - ix1) * (iy2 - iy1)
    return inter / inner_area if inner_area > 0 else 0

def _drop_contained_boxes(
    boxes_kinds: list[tuple[tuple[int, int, int, int], str]],
    containment_thr: float = 0.85,
) -> list[tuple[tuple[int, int, int, int], str]]:
    """Drop boxes that sit almost entirely inside a larger box — the bigger
    box's OCR already covers that content, so keeping both duplicates it."""
    areas = [((x2 - x1) * (y2 - y1), box, kind) for (box, kind) in boxes_kinds for x1, y1, x2, y2 in [box]]
    areas.sort(key=lambda t: t[0], reverse=True)  # largest first

    kept: list[tuple[tuple, str]] = []
    for area, box, kind in areas:
        if any(_containment_ratio(box, kb) > containment_thr for kb, _ in kept):
            continue  # this box is basically inside an already-kept larger box — skip it
        kept.append((box, kind))
    return kept

Wire it in right after your existing dedup step, since they solve different problems (duplicate detections of the same object vs. nested detections at different granularities):

def detect(pages: list[Page], cfg: dict) -> list[Region]:
    ...
    for page in pages:
        ...
        deduped = _dedup_boxes(raw, iou_thr=dedup_iou)
        deduped = _drop_contained_boxes(deduped, containment_thr=cfg["layout"].get("containment_thr", 0.85))
        for (x1, y1, x2, y2), kind in deduped:
            regions.append(Region(page_id=page.id, bbox=(x1, y1, x2, y2), kind=kind))
    ...

Worth sanity-checking containment_thr=0.85 on a few pages where a real figure contains a small distinct label vs. a text block that fully contains a sub-formula — you want to keep genuinely distinct nested content (e.g., a figure with an inset diagram) while dropping true redundant sub-boxes like Image 2's formula fragments. If you see it dropping something that should've stayed separate, raise the threshold toward 0.95 so only near-total containment triggers the drop.

Done

Good question — worth checking before you sink time into it. Chunking, embedding, and indexing depend only on ocr.transcribe()'s output (the Chunk objects with real transcribed text) — none of them touch QA pairs. So there's no pipeline dependency blocking you: index/chunk.py, index/embed.py, index/store.py are exactly the sequence you should be working on next while your partner finishes ocr.py, and QA generation is a separate, independent track needed for kb_demo.ipynb (to demonstrate retrieval actually works) and likely for eval later.

Two practical things worth doing given your partner isn't done yet:

Build chunk/embed/store against a small hand-made stub of Chunk objects first (2-3 fake chunks with real Bangla text you type in yourself), so you're not blocked waiting for real OCR output to test your code.

Generate QA pairs from your ground-truth text, not from OCR output. Your run_ingest.py debug code already references data/ground-truth-ocr/{doc_id}/{page_stem}.txt — since that ground truth already exists independent of your partner's OCR work, generating QA pairs from it means you're not blocked by OCR bugs, and you get clean pairs unaffected by transcription errors (useful for kb_demo.ipynb's "does retrieval actually work" demo, since noisy OCR-derived QA pairs would confound whether a wrong answer is a retrieval failure or an OCR failure).

# scripts/generate_qa_pairs.py
"""Generate one QA pair per page from ground-truth OCR text (independent of the
live OCR pipeline — safe to run in parallel with vision/ocr.py development)."""
from __future__ import annotations
import json
import os
from pathlib import Path
import anthropic

DATA_ROOT = Path("data")
GT_DIR = DATA_ROOT / "ground-truth-ocr"
OUT_PATH = Path("data") / "qa_pairs.jsonl"

client = anthropic.Anthropic(api_key=os.environ["ANTHROPIC_API_KEY"])

PROMPT = """তুমি একজন সহকারী যিনি একটি বাংলা কৃষি বিষয়ক পাঠ্য থেকে একটি প্রশ্ন-উত্তর জোড়া তৈরি করবে।
নিচের পাঠ্যাংশ পড়ে এমন একটি প্রশ্ন তৈরি করো যার উত্তর সরাসরি এই পাঠ্যে পাওয়া যায়, এবং সংক্ষিপ্ত সঠিক উত্তর দাও।

শুধুমাত্র নিচের JSON ফরম্যাটে উত্তর দাও, অন্য কিছু লিখো না:
{{"question": "...", "answer": "..."}}

পাঠ্য:
\"\"\"
{page_text}
\"\"\"
"""

def generate_qa(page_text: str) -> dict | None:
    if len(page_text.strip()) < 40:          # skip near-empty / blank pages
        return None
    resp = client.messages.create(
        model="claude-sonnet-4-6",
        max_tokens=300,
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": PROMPT.format(page_text=page_text[:3000])}],
    )
    raw = resp.content[0].text.strip()
    try:
        qa = json.loads(raw)
        if "question" in qa and "answer" in qa:
            return qa
    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        print(f"  [skip] could not parse model output: {raw[:120]}")
    return None

def main():
    pairs = []
    doc_dirs = sorted(p for p in GT_DIR.iterdir() if p.is_dir())
    for doc_dir in doc_dirs:
        doc_id = doc_dir.name
        for txt_path in sorted(doc_dir.glob("*.txt")):
            page_id = txt_path.stem
            page_text = txt_path.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
            print(f"{doc_id} / {page_id} ...")
            qa = generate_qa(page_text)
            if qa is None:
                continue
            pairs.append({
                "id": f"{doc_id}_{page_id}",
                "doc_id": doc_id,
                "page_id": page_id,
                "question": qa["question"],
                "answer": qa["answer"],
            })

    OUT_PATH.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    with open(OUT_PATH, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        for p in pairs:
            f.write(json.dumps(p, ensure_ascii=False) + "\n")
    print(f"\nWrote {len(pairs)} QA pairs to {OUT_PATH}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Notes:

client.messages.create here uses the Anthropic API directly — swap to whatever provider your configs/config.yaml's llm section actually specifies if it's different (OpenAI, etc.); I don't have that file in front of me right now so double-check before running.

Skipping pages under 40 characters avoids wasting calls on blank/near-blank pages (you mentioned 749 pages after removing blanks, but some may still be sparse — title pages, single-line captions).

749 pages × 1 API call each is a real cost/time commitment — consider testing on 10-15 pages first (doc_dirs[:1], slice the glob) before running the full corpus, and check a handful of the generated Q/A pairs by eye for quality before trusting all of them in kb_demo.ipynb.

One thing to verify before running this broadly: confirm data/ground-truth-ocr/{doc_id}/{page_id}.txt actually exists for all 749 pages and matches the doc_id/page_id naming your Page objects use elsewhere — if your ground truth was only built for a subset (e.g., just for grading_kit/heldout_pages/), you'll want to point this script at whatever wider ground-truth text does exist rather than assuming full coverage.

8/14/2026 1:01:05

Architected evaluation metrics for retrieval system performance assessment.

Architected evaluation metrics for retrieval system performance assessment.

Done

Good pivot — QA generation was actually the wrong track; what you need for a retrieval eval is simpler: queries paired with the correct source page, not full question-answer pairs. That's because retrieval evaluation only needs to check "did the system return the chunk that actually contains the answer" — you don't need a generated answer at all, and skipping that saves you API calls too.

What retrieval eval actually needs

A small set of (query, correct_page_id) pairs — a query can just be a short factual question about something on that page.

Run each query through your retriever, get back top-k chunks.

Check whether any of the top-k chunks came from the correct page.

Compute standard metrics over the set: Hit@k / Recall@k, MRR (mean reciprocal rank), optionally Precision@k.

You can reuse most of the generation script from before, just simplified to skip the "answer" field and produce a query, not a full QA pair — cheaper and exactly what retrieval eval needs.

# scripts/generate_eval_queries.py
"""Generate one retrieval-eval query per sampled page, from ground-truth OCR text.
Independent of the live OCR pipeline — safe to run while ocr.py is still in progress."""
from __future__ import annotations
import json
import os
import random
from pathlib import Path
import anthropic

GT_DIR = Path("data") / "ground-truth-ocr"
OUT_PATH = Path("data") / "eval_queries.jsonl"
N_PAGES = 60  # sample size; bump up later if time allows

client = anthropic.Anthropic(api_key=os.environ["ANTHROPIC_API_KEY"])

PROMPT = """নিচের বাংলা পাঠ্যাংশ পড়ে এমন একটি সংক্ষিপ্ত, নির্দিষ্ট প্রশ্ন তৈরি করো যার উত্তর
শুধুমাত্র এই পাঠ্যাংশেই পাওয়া যায় (অন্য কোনো সাধারণ পাতায় নয়)।
শুধু JSON আকারে উত্তর দাও, অন্য কিছু নয়: {{"query": "..."}}

পাঠ্য:
\"\"\"
{page_text}
\"\"\"
"""

def generate_query(page_text: str) -> str | None:
    if len(page_text.strip()) < 40:
        return None
    resp = client.messages.create(
        model="claude-sonnet-4-6",
        max_tokens=150,
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": PROMPT.format(page_text=page_text[:3000])}],
    )
    raw = resp.content[0].text.strip()
    try:
        return json.loads(raw)["query"]
    except (json.JSONDecodeError, KeyError):
        return None

def main():
    all_pages = []
    for doc_dir in sorted(p for p in GT_DIR.iterdir() if p.is_dir()):
        for txt_path in sorted(doc_dir.glob("*.txt")):
            all_pages.append((doc_dir.name, txt_path))

    random.seed(42)
    sample = random.sample(all_pages, min(N_PAGES, len(all_pages)))

    out = []
    for doc_id, txt_path in sample:
        page_id = txt_path.stem
        text = txt_path.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
        print(f"{doc_id}/{page_id} ...")
        q = generate_query(text)
        if q:
            out.append({"query": q, "gold_doc_id": doc_id, "gold_page_id": page_id})

    OUT_PATH.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    with open(OUT_PATH, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        for row in out:
            f.write(json.dumps(row, ensure_ascii=False) + "\n")
    print(f"\nWrote {len(out)} eval queries to {OUT_PATH}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

The actual evaluation script

# scripts/eval_retrieval.py
"""Recall@k / Hit@k / MRR over data/eval_queries.jsonl against the built index."""
from __future__ import annotations
import json
from pathlib import Path
from src.doc_agent import config
from src.doc_agent.retrieval.retriever import Retriever

QUERIES_PATH = Path("data") / "eval_queries.jsonl"
K_VALUES = [1, 3, 5]

def load_queries():
    with open(QUERIES_PATH, encoding="utf-8") as f:
        return [json.loads(line) for line in f]

def chunk_matches_gold(chunk, gold_doc_id, gold_page_id) -> bool:
    # adjust field names to match your Chunk contract — likely doc_id + page_ids
    return chunk.doc_id == gold_doc_id and gold_page_id in getattr(chunk, "page_ids", [])

def main():
    cfg = config.load()
    retriever = Retriever(cfg)
    queries = load_queries()

    hits_at_k = {k: 0 for k in K_VALUES}
    reciprocal_ranks = []

    for row in queries:
        results = retriever.retrieve(row["query"], top_k=max(K_VALUES))  # adjust method name if different
        rank = None
        for i, chunk in enumerate(results, start=1):
            if chunk_matches_gold(chunk, row["gold_doc_id"], row["gold_page_id"]):
                rank = i
                break
        reciprocal_ranks.append(1.0 / rank if rank else 0.0)
        for k in K_VALUES:
            if rank is not None and rank <= k:
                hits_at_k[k] += 1

    n = len(queries)
    print(f"Evaluated {n} queries\n")
    for k in K_VALUES:
        print(f"Recall@{k} (Hit@{k}): {hits_at_k[k] / n:.3f}")
    print(f"MRR: {sum(reciprocal_ranks) / n:.3f}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Things to double-check against your actual code before running:

Retriever's real method name/signature (retrieve(query, top_k) is a guess — check retrieval/retriever.py).

Chunk's actual fields (chunk.doc_id, chunk.page_ids) — match whatever contracts.py defines.

Whether gold_page_id should be compared as string vs int, matching however Page.id/page_stem is formatted elsewhere in your pipeline.

For kb_demo.ipynb specifically: print a small table of a handful of queries with their retrieved top-1 chunk text next to the ground-truth page text, so a grader can visually confirm retrieval is working, then show the aggregate Recall@k/MRR numbers below that as the quantitative summary. That combination (qualitative examples + quantitative metrics) is exactly what Section 5 wants to see.

what if a page has multiple chunks and it retrieved the wrong chunk?

Assistant:

8/14/2026 1:08:02

Good question — that's a real correctness issue, not just an edge case, and it means my chunk_matches_gold check from before is too loose. Comparing only doc_id + page_id treats any chunk from the right page as a hit, even if it's the wrong chunk on that page — which would silently mask exactly the failure you're describing.

Fix the eval script's matching, not the model

The cleanest fix is to make the gold label a chunk-level ID, not a page-level one — so eval correctly distinguishes "retrieved the right paragraph" from "retrieved a different paragraph that happens to share a page."

1. Change generate_eval_queries.py to target a chunk, not a whole page. Since your chunker will split each page's OCR text into multiple chunks, the cleanest approach: run this after index/chunk.py exists, sample from actual Chunk objects instead of raw ground-truth page text, and record chunk.id as the gold label:

# scripts/generate_eval_queries.py (revised gold target)
...
def main():
    cfg = config.load()
    # load your already-built chunks (however store.py / chunk.py exposes them)
    all_chunks = load_all_chunks(cfg)   # replace with your actual accessor

    random.seed(42)
    sample = random.sample(all_chunks, min(N_CHUNKS, len(all_chunks)))

    out = []
    for chunk in sample:
        print(f"chunk {chunk.id} ...")
        q = generate_query(chunk.text)
        if q:
            out.append({
                "query": q,
                "gold_chunk_id": chunk.id,          # <-- chunk-level gold now
                "gold_doc_id": chunk.doc_id,
                "gold_page_ids": chunk.page_ids,
            })
    ...

2. Change the eval matching to compare chunk IDs directly:

# scripts/eval_retrieval.py
def chunk_matches_gold(retrieved_chunk, gold_chunk_id) -> bool:
    return retrieved_chunk.id == gold_chunk_id

That alone fixes the over-counting — a hit now only counts if the retriever surfaced the exact chunk the query was generated from, not just any chunk from the same page.

Worth reporting both levels, not just chunk-level

For your robust-and-reliable NFR story specifically, it's actually informative to report both chunk-level and page-level Recall@k side by side in kb_demo.ipynb / eval_report.md:

Chunk-level Recall@k — strict, "did we get the exact right passage."

Page-level Recall@k — looser, "did we at least get something from the right page" (using your original doc_id + page_id check).

The gap between the two numbers is itself a useful, honest diagnostic: if page-level recall is much higher than chunk-level, that tells you your chunking granularity is the weak point (right neighborhood, wrong exact passage) rather than the embedding/retrieval logic being broken. That's a much better thing to say in Section 5's "worst failure" cell than just a single blended number — it shows you understand why retrieval sometimes gets the wrong chunk, not just that it happens.

# scripts/eval_retrieval.py — reporting both
hits_chunk = {k: 0 for k in K_VALUES}
hits_page = {k: 0 for k in K_VALUES}

for row in queries:
    results = retriever.retrieve(row["query"], top_k=max(K_VALUES))
    chunk_rank = next((i for i, c in enumerate(results, 1) if c.id == row["gold_chunk_id"]), None)
    page_rank = next((i for i, c in enumerate(results, 1)
                       if c.doc_id == row["gold_doc_id"] and set(c.page_ids) & set(row["gold_page_ids"])), None)
    for k in K_VALUES:
        if chunk_rank and chunk_rank <= k:
            hits_chunk[k] += 1
        if page_rank and page_rank <= k:
            hits_page[k] += 1

n = len(queries)
for k in K_VALUES:
    print(f"Chunk-level Recall@{k}: {hits_chunk[k]/n:.3f}   Page-level Recall@{k}: {hits_page[k]/n:.3f}")

One dependency to flag: since this now requires real chunks to exist, you can't run this eval until index/chunk.py (and ideally embed.py/store.py) are working — so it's a good task to slot in right after you finish those, before moving to retrieval/agent work.

8/14/2026 1:18:53

which chunking strategy should I follow?

Assistant:

8/14/2026 1:19:17

Evaluated chunking methodologies and synthesized optimal strategy recommendation.

Evaluated chunking methodologies and synthesized optimal strategy recommendation.

Done

Given what you're actually working with — Bangla text, OCR output that's noisy in places, and regions already typed as text/heading/table/figure — I'd steer away from generic fixed-size chunking (e.g. "every 500 characters") and go with structure-aware chunking that respects your existing region boundaries, then merges/splits within that structure. Reasoning:

Why not naive fixed-size chunking

Splitting by raw character/token count ignores your region boundaries entirely — it could cut a heading away from the paragraph it introduces, or split a table row mid-cell. Since you already did the hard work of getting Region.kind right in Stage 2, throwing that structure away at chunk time wastes it.

Recommended strategy: region-aware chunking with a heading-anchor rule

1. Sort regions into reading order first. Your detect() doesn't guarantee top-to-bottom order (we noted this back when discussing C16/layout-as-graph) — sort by (y1, x1) per page before chunking, or your paragraphs will come out scrambled.

2. Chunk boundaries follow region kind:

heading → attach to the start of the next chunk, never stand alone and never get split from what follows it. A heading with no body text is a useless, unretrievable chunk.

text → merge consecutive text regions into one chunk up to a max size (e.g. 800–1200 characters — tune against your actual embedding model's context), splitting only at a paragraph boundary if a single region is too long. Don't split mid-sentence — for Bangla, split on । (dari, the Bangla sentence terminator), not on Latin ..

table → each table stays its own chunk, never merged with neighboring text. As discussed earlier, since you're not doing table-structure recognition, treat it as one flat text blob rather than trying to reconstruct rows/columns — but keep it isolated so a table's dense list of names doesn't dilute a narrative paragraph's embedding, and vice versa.

figure → skipped already at the OCR stage, so nothing to chunk.

3. Add small overlap between adjacent text chunks (e.g. last 1 sentence of chunk N repeated at the start of chunk N+1) — this is a standard RAG chunking trick that reduces the "answer sentence landed right on a chunk boundary and got split across two chunks" failure mode, which matters for your robust-and-reliable NFR since evidence gating depends on chunks being self-contained enough to judge.

4. Keep page provenance on every chunk — you already need page_ids on Chunk for citation/evidence-gating anyway (this ties directly into your NFR's "evidence-gated abstention" requirement — the agent needs to point back to a real page).

# index/chunk.py — sketch
import re

def _sort_reading_order(regions: list[Region]) -> list[Region]:
    return sorted(regions, key=lambda r: (r.bbox[1], r.bbox[0]))  # (y1, x1)

def _split_sentences_bangla(text: str) -> list[str]:
    # split on Bangla dari (।) and also '?'/'!' as fallbacks; keep the delimiter
    parts = re.split(r'(?<=[।?!])\s+', text.strip())
    return [p for p in parts if p]

def split(text_chunks: list[Chunk], cfg: dict) -> list[Chunk]:
    max_chars = cfg["chunk"].get("max_chars", 1000)
    overlap_sentences = cfg["chunk"].get("overlap_sentences", 1)

    # group incoming OCR chunks by page, in reading order
    by_page: dict[str, list[Chunk]] = {}
    for c in text_chunks:
        for pid in c.page_ids:
            by_page.setdefault(pid, []).append(c)

    out: list[Chunk] = []
    for page_id, page_chunks in by_page.items():
        buffer_sentences: list[str] = []
        buffer_len = 0
        pending_heading = ""

        def flush():
            nonlocal buffer_sentences, buffer_len
            if buffer_sentences:
                out.append(Chunk(
                    id=..., doc_id=page_chunks[0].doc_id,
                    text=" ".join(buffer_sentences), page_ids=[page_id],
                ))
                # keep last N sentences for overlap into the next chunk
                buffer_sentences = buffer_sentences[-overlap_sentences:] if overlap_sentences else []
                buffer_len = sum(len(s) for s in buffer_sentences)

        for c in page_chunks:  # assumes caller already sorted regions into reading order upstream
            kind = getattr(c, "kind", "text")
            if kind == "table":
                flush()
                out.append(c)  # table stays intact, its own chunk
                continue
            if kind == "heading":
                pending_heading = c.text.strip()
                continue

            sentences = _split_sentences_bangla(c.text)
            if pending_heading:
                sentences = [pending_heading] + sentences
                pending_heading = ""

            for s in sentences:
                if buffer_len + len(s) > max_chars and buffer_sentences:
                    flush()
                buffer_sentences.append(s)
                buffer_len += len(s)
        flush()

    return out

Two things worth deciding before you lock this in:

max_chars=1000 is a starting guess, not a rule — check it against your embedding model's actual token limit and typical chunk-vs-query length ratio. Too small and you lose context; too large and unrelated content gets embedded together, hurting retrieval precision.

Don't forget the Unicode NFC normalization step we discussed for Bangla conjuncts — run it before sentence-splitting, since a decomposed conjunct could otherwise confuse the । regex or produce visually-identical-but-differently-encoded chunks.

Once you have this running, your chunk-level vs. page-level Recall@k comparison from the eval script will directly tell you whether max_chars is well-tuned — if page-level recall is notably higher than chunk-level, that's your signal the chunks are either too big (burying the answer among unrelated content) or too small (splitting the answer across a boundary), and worth revisiting.

What reranker should we use?

Assistant:

8/14/2026 2:01:31

For your setup — multilingual (Bangla) corpus, small-medium chunk count (749 pages worth), likely CPU/limited-GPU — a lightweight cross-encoder reranker is the right fit, not something heavy like Cohere's hosted API (extra cost/network dependency + English-first, not ideal for Bangla) or a full LLM-as-reranker (slow, expensive at scale).

Recommended: BGE-reranker-v2-m3

pip install FlagEmbedding

from FlagEmbedding import FlagReranker

reranker = FlagReranker("BAAI/bge-reranker-v2-m3", use_fp16=True)  # fp16 fine on GPU; drop use_fp16 on CPU

def rerank(query: str, candidates: list[Chunk], top_k: int = 5) -> list[Chunk]:
    pairs = [[query, c.text] for c in candidates]
    scores = reranker.compute_score(pairs, normalize=True)
    ranked = sorted(zip(candidates, scores), key=lambda x: x[1], reverse=True)
    return [c for c, _ in ranked[:top_k]]

Why this one specifically:

Explicitly multilingual (the "m3" lineage is trained across 100+ languages including Bangla) — unlike most English-only rerankers (e.g. ms-marco-MiniLM), which would silently underperform on Bangla queries/chunks without you necessarily noticing until eval numbers looked off.

Cross-encoder, not bi-encoder — it jointly scores (query, chunk) pairs rather than comparing independent embeddings, which is why reranking after your initial retrieval catches relevance nuances your embedding-based index/embed.py + FAISS retrieval alone misses. This is the standard two-stage retrieval pattern: fast approximate retrieval (embeddings) → precise reranking (cross-encoder) on a small candidate set.

Runs locally, no API cost — matters given you're already managing multiple heavy local dependencies (DocLayout-YOLO, Tesseract); adding a paid reranking API is one more moving part and cost line you don't need.

Reasonably light — smaller than full BGE-M3 embedding models, practical on CPU for reranking a top-20-or-so candidate list per query (don't rerank your whole corpus — only rerank what your Stage-5 retriever already narrowed down).

Where it plugs into your pipeline

This belongs in retrieval/retriever.py (Stage 5), as a second pass after FAISS similarity search, not in the indexing stages:

# retrieval/retriever.py
class Retriever:
    def __init__(self, cfg):
        self.store = ...        # your existing FAISS/store.py load
        self.embedder = ...     # your existing embed.py model
        self.reranker = FlagReranker("BAAI/bge-reranker-v2-m3", use_fp16=False)
        self.rerank_top_n = cfg["retrieval"].get("rerank_candidates", 20)

    def retrieve(self, query: str, top_k: int = 5) -> list[Chunk]:
        query_vec = self.embedder.encode([query])
        candidates = self.store.search(query_vec, k=self.rerank_top_n)  # wide net first
        return rerank(query, candidates, top_k=top_k)                   # narrow, precise second pass

One thing worth checking once you have this wired up: rerun your chunk-level vs. page-level Recall@k eval with and without reranking on the same query set. That before/after comparison is good, concrete evidence for design_choices.md's retrieval row — if reranking meaningfully improves chunk-level recall, that's a strong justification; if it barely moves the needle given your corpus size, it's honest to say so and note the added latency/complexity cost against a modest gain (also a legitimate design tradeoff to report).

Architected dual-pass OCR strategy and endorsed BGE-M3 embeddings.

Architected dual-pass OCR strategy and endorsed BGE-M3 embeddings.

Done

1. Ben-only Tesseract garbling English → corrector hallucination

The root problem is upstream of the corrector: once _tesseract_region() runs with lang="ben", English glyphs get force-mapped onto the nearest Bangla character shapes at the recognition step — by the time that garbled text reaches the mT5 corrector, the original English content is already gone, not just misspelled. That's why ben+eng doesn't just "let English through" cleanly either — mixing two language models in one pass increases confusion on the Bangla majority text too, which is why your corpus-wide CER/WER got worse with it. And since your corrector was almost certainly fine-tuned on Bangla-only OCR-fix pairs, feeding it plausible-looking-but-wrong Bangla glyph sequences is exactly the kind of out-of-distribution input that makes seq2seq correctors hallucinate — it has no signal telling it "this was actually English," so it just produces its best guess at some Bangla word.

Fix the root cause, not the correction step: decide the script per word, before deciding which OCR pass to trust. Use ben's own word bounding boxes (image_to_data) — those boxes are usually still spatially correct even when the characters inside them are wrong — then re-OCR each word crop with both ben and eng, and keep whichever gives higher confidence for that specific word. This means English tokens actually get read as English, never garbled into fake-Bangla in the first place.

# vision/ocr.py — additions

def _word_boxes(img_bgr: np.ndarray, lang: str, psm: int) -> list[dict]:
    """Get word-level boxes + text + confidence for one region crop."""
    rgb = cv2.cvtColor(img_bgr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)
    data = pytesseract.image_to_data(rgb, lang=lang, config=f"--psm {psm}", output_type=pytesseract.Output.DICT)
    words = []
    for i, text in enumerate(data["text"]):
        if not text.strip():
            continue
        words.append({
            "text": text,
            "conf": float(data["conf"][i]),
            "left": data["left"][i], "top": data["top"][i],
            "width": data["width"][i], "height": data["height"][i],
            "line_num": data["line_num"][i], "word_num": data["word_num"][i],
        })
    return words

def _resolve_mixed_script_line(img_bgr: np.ndarray, psm: int) -> str:
    """Get word boxes from 'ben', then re-read each word crop with both 'ben'
    and 'eng', keeping whichever has higher confidence for that word. Fixes
    English tokens being force-mapped into garbled Bangla glyphs."""
    ben_words = _word_boxes(img_bgr, lang="ben", psm=psm)
    resolved: list[tuple[int, int, str]] = []  # (line_num, word_num, chosen_text)

    for w in ben_words:
        x, y, cw, ch = w["left"], w["top"], w["width"], w["height"]
        pad = 2
        crop = img_bgr[max(0, y - pad):y + ch + pad, max(0, x - pad):x + cw + pad]
        if crop.size == 0:
            continue
        rgb = cv2.cvtColor(crop, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)
        ben_txt = pytesseract.image_to_string(rgb, lang="ben", config="--psm 8").strip()
        eng_txt = pytesseract.image_to_string(rgb, lang="eng", config="--psm 8").strip()
        ben_data = pytesseract.image_to_data(rgb, lang="ben", config="--psm 8", output_type=pytesseract.Output.DICT)
        eng_data = pytesseract.image_to_data(rgb, lang="eng", config="--psm 8", output_type=pytesseract.Output.DICT)
        ben_conf = max([c for c in ben_data["conf"] if c != -1], default=-1)
        eng_conf = max([c for c in eng_data["conf"] if c != -1], default=-1)
        chosen = eng_txt if eng_conf > ben_conf and eng_txt else (ben_txt or w["text"])
        resolved.append((w["line_num"], w["word_num"], chosen))

    lines: dict[int, list[tuple[int, str]]] = {}
    for line_num, word_num, txt in resolved:
        lines.setdefault(line_num, []).append((word_num, txt))
    out_lines = [" ".join(t for _, t in sorted(words)) for _, words in sorted(lines.items())]
    return "\n".join(out_lines)

Swap _tesseract_region()'s call for this in the region loop — it's more expensive (roughly 2× OCR calls per word instead of one per region), so worth gating it behind a config flag and only applying it where mixed script is actually likely rather than on every region:

# in the Pass 1 loop, replace the single _tesseract_region call:
if ocr_cfg.get("dual_lang_word_level", True):
    raw_text = _resolve_mixed_script_line(crop, psm)
else:
    raw_text = _tesseract_region(crop, lang=lang, psm=psm)

Second half of the fix — protect resolved English spans from the mT5 corrector, since even with correct OCR text, the corrector could still "fix" a real English word (e.g. "sugar beet", "casuarina" from that plants-usage table) into Bangla nonsense if it wasn't trained to recognize legitimate embedded English:

# vision/ocr.py

_BANGLA_RANGE = (0x0980, 0x09FF)

def _is_bangla_word(word: str) -> bool:
    bangla_chars = sum(1 for ch in word if _BANGLA_RANGE[0] <= ord(ch) <= _BANGLA_RANGE[1])
    return bangla_chars > len(word) / 2 if word else False

def _mask_non_bangla(line: str) -> tuple[str, dict[str, str]]:
    """Replace non-Bangla words with placeholder tokens before correction,
    so the corrector never sees (and never 'fixes') real English content."""
    tokens = line.split()
    masked_tokens, placeholders = [], {}
    for i, tok in enumerate(tokens):
        if tok.strip() and not _is_bangla_word(tok):
            key = f"__EN{i}__"
            placeholders[key] = tok
            masked_tokens.append(key)
        else:
            masked_tokens.append(tok)
    return " ".join(masked_tokens), placeholders

def _unmask(corrected_line: str, placeholders: dict[str, str]) -> str:
    for key, original in placeholders.items():
        corrected_line = corrected_line.replace(key, original)
    return corrected_line

Apply masking right before you feed flat_lines into _get_corrector(cfg).correct_batch(...), and unmask right after:

masked_lines, all_placeholders = [], []
for ln in flat_lines:
    m, ph = _mask_non_bangla(ln)
    masked_lines.append(m)
    all_placeholders.append(ph)

corrected_flat = _get_corrector(cfg).correct_batch(masked_lines, batch_size=batch_size) \
    if use_corrector and flat_lines else flat_lines

corrected_flat = [_unmask(c, ph) for c, ph in zip(corrected_flat, all_placeholders)]

Worth checking on a handful of pages: the Bharater-Krishi-Babyasthar-Parichay table page you showed earlier (essential oil / dye / timber usage table, mixing Bangla with eucalyptus, henna, rosewood, walnut) is a good stress test — confirm those English botanical names now survive intact through both OCR and correction. Also worth reporting your held-out CER/WER (you already track Krishi-Darpan as held-out) before/after this change — that's a clean, quantifiable improvement to cite in eval_report.md.

2. Embedding — BGE-M3 is a solid choice, and it's already half-adopted

Good instinct, and notably chunk.py already uses AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("BAAI/bge-m3") for token counting — which means your chunk sizes (chunk_tokens=256, chunk_max_tokens=512) are already measured in the exact tokenizer the embedding model will consume. That's a real design win worth keeping: no silent mismatch between "how big I think this chunk is" and "how big the embedder thinks it is."

Reasons BGE-M3 is the right call here specifically:

Genuinely multilingual (100+ languages, Bangla included) — same rationale as the reranker.

8192 token context — comfortably covers your chunk_max_tokens=512 cap with huge headroom, so no truncation risk.

Supports dense + sparse + multi-vector (ColBERT-style) in one model. For A2, I'd use dense only — simplest to wire into FAISS, and matches your index/store.py contract expecting fixed-dim vectors. But it's worth flagging as a natural A3 extension: BGE-M3's sparse output is essentially a learned lexical/BM25-like signal, which would specifically help with the exact-term-matching problem you're already fighting in OCR — English botanical/technical terms (eucalyptus, casuarina, sugar beet) that dense embeddings can blur together but exact sparse matching catches precisely. Not needed now, but a nice, honest thing to note in design_choices.md as future work that ties your OCR and retrieval design choices together.

# index/embed.py
from __future__ import annotations
import numpy as np
from FlagEmbedding import BGEM3FlagModel
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

_MODEL = None

def _get_model(device: str):
    global _MODEL
    if _MODEL is None:
        _MODEL = BGEM3FlagModel("BAAI/bge-m3", use_fp16=(device != "cpu"), device=device)
    return _MODEL

def encode(chunks: list[Chunk], cfg: dict) -> np.ndarray:
    embed_cfg = cfg.get("embed", {})
    device = cfg.get("device", "cpu")
    batch_size = int(embed_cfg.get("batch_size", 16))
    max_length = int(embed_cfg.get("max_length", 512))  # matches chunk_max_tokens

    model = _get_model(device)
    texts = [c.text for c in chunks]

    output = model.encode(
        texts,
        batch_size=batch_size,
        max_length=max_length,
        return_dense=True,
        return_sparse=False,     # dense-only for A2; sparse available later without re-embedding
        return_colbert_vecs=False,
    )
    dense_vecs = output["dense_vecs"]  # shape: (n_chunks, 1024)
    return np.asarray(dense_vecs, dtype=np.float32)

Two things to check/update given this choice:

configs/config.yaml's embedding dim needs to be 1024 (BGE-M3's dense output size) — if it's still set from an earlier discussion of paraphrase-multilingual-mpnet-base-v2 (768-dim), store.py's FAISS index will be built with the wrong dimensionality and silently break or error at insert time.

Cache embeddings per chunk, following the same pattern already established in chunk.py/ocr.py (page/region-level JSON caches) — re-embedding all 749 pages' worth of chunks every debug run of store.build() is wasted GPU/CPU time you don't need to pay twice.

One more consistency point since your reranker discussion used BAAI/bge-reranker-v2-m3 — that's a different model from BAAI/bge-m3 (embedding) despite the similar name; both are needed, they serve different stages (embed → retrieve, then rerank on the retrieved candidates), so no conflict there, just worth being clear about in your pipeline diagram so it doesn't read as a duplicate/mistake.

8/14/2026 5:52:04

Architected detection strategy for unreliable output patterns.

Architected detection strategy for unreliable output patterns.

Done

Good question — you don't want to guess up front, you want a cheap signal from the ben-only pass itself that flags "this region is suspect, worth the expensive dual-pass." Two signals combine well, and both come nearly free from data you're already computing:

1. Tesseract's own per-word confidence from the ben pass. When ben is forced onto real English glyphs, it tends to produce low-confidence guesses — the character shapes don't match Bangla glyph patterns well, so Tesseract's internal scoring reflects that uncertainty even before you know it's wrong.

2. Character-shape irregularity in the ben output itself. Force-mapped English-as-Bangla often produces "words" with unusual glyph combinations — e.g., a suspiciously high proportion of Bangla conjunct/diacritic marks with no vowel signs, or very short "words" that don't look like real Bangla morphology. A simpler, more robust proxy: just check average word confidence per region — you don't need this second signal to work well on its own, it's a shortcut to a decision you can make even more simply than that.

Given you already have _word_boxes() from the fix I gave you, the simplest reliable gate is: run the cheap single-pass ben OCR first (as you already do), then check if that region's average word confidence falls below a threshold — only then re-run the expensive dual-pass resolution.

# vision/ocr.py

def _needs_dual_lang_check(img_bgr: np.ndarray, lang: str, psm: int,
                            conf_thr: float = 55.0, min_words: int = 1) -> bool:
    """Cheap gate: run a single ben pass with confidence scores. If average
    word confidence is low, this region likely has non-Bangla content Tesseract
    is struggling to force-fit — worth the expensive dual-pass resolution.
    If confidence is already high, skip it and trust the fast single pass."""
    rgb = cv2.cvtColor(img_bgr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)
    data = pytesseract.image_to_data(rgb, lang=lang, config=f"--psm {psm}", output_type=pytesseract.Output.DICT)
    confs = [c for t, c in zip(data["text"], data["conf"]) if t.strip() and c != -1]
    if len(confs) < min_words:
        return False  # nothing to go on — don't pay for the expensive pass on empty/near-empty regions
    avg_conf = sum(confs) / len(confs)
    return avg_conf < conf_thr

Wire it into the Pass 1 loop as the actual gate, instead of an always-on flag or an always-off one:

# in the Pass 1 loop:
for region in page_regions[key]:
    psm = 7 if region.kind == "heading" else 6
    crop = _crop(image_path, region.bbox)

    if ocr_cfg.get("dual_lang_word_level", True) and _needs_dual_lang_check(crop, lang, psm):
        raw_text = _resolve_mixed_script_line(crop, psm)
    else:
        raw_text = _tesseract_region(crop, lang=lang, psm=psm)

This means:

Regions that are clearly pure Bangla prose (the vast majority of your 749 pages) stay on the fast, cheap single-pass path — no wasted compute.

Regions where ben is struggling — which, based on what you've shown me, correlates strongly with your table pages (essential oils, dyes, timber names mixing Bangla labels with English/Latin botanical terms) and any figure captions citing English-named sources (e.g. "[ L. S. S. KUMAR মহাশয়ের সৌজন্যে ]", "[ H. R. ARAKERI মহাশয়ের সৌজন্যে ]") — automatically get flagged and upgraded to the expensive dual-pass without you having to manually mark which pages/regions are mixed-script.

Practical tuning step: run this on a sample of ~20 pages including your known mixed-script table pages (the plant-usage table, the source-attribution captions) and a similar number of pure-Bangla-prose pages, print avg_conf for each region, and pick conf_thr at the value that separates the two groups cleanly. That's the same "eyeball a sample before trusting it on 749 pages" approach you've been using throughout — and it's cheap here since it's just printing numbers, not re-running heavy models.

One thing worth logging once this is running for real: track how many regions actually trigger the dual-pass across the full corpus (a simple counter alongside your existing [timing] prints). If it's triggering on, say, 90% of regions, your threshold is too permissive and you're not actually saving the compute this gate was meant to save — that's a quick sanity check before committing to a conf_thr value in design_choices.md.

This is a version mismatch, not a code bug — transformers recently changed its model-init signature (dtype is passed differently than older FlagEmbedding/transformers pairings expect), and FlagEmbedding's internal call to AutoModel.from_pretrained is using a dtype kwarg your installed transformers version's XLMRobertaModel.__init__ doesn't accept. This is a known compatibility gap between FlagEmbedding and newer transformers releases, not something wrong in your embed.py.

Fix: pin transformers to a version FlagEmbedding was actually tested against, rather than whatever the latest resolved version is:

pip install "transformers==4.44.2" --break-system-packages

(If you're not on the container and this is your own venv, drop --break-system-packages.) This version predates the dtype-kwarg change and is a version FlagEmbedding/BGE-M3 loading is commonly verified against. After downgrading, rerun scripts/run_ingest.py — the AutoModel.from_pretrained call inside BGEM3FlagModel.__init__ should succeed.

If pinning transformers breaks something else you rely on elsewhere in the pipeline (e.g. if ocr.py's AutoModelForSeq2SeqLM/mT5 corrector or chunk.py's AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("BAAI/bge-m3") needs a newer transformers API), the safer alternative is pinning FlagEmbedding itself to a version that matches your current transformers, since that's the actual mismatched pair:

pip install -U FlagEmbedding --break-system-packages

Try the transformers downgrade first since it's the more surgical fix — one dependency pinned instead of touching whatever FlagEmbedding version constraints ripple out.

One thing worth doing once it loads successfully: freeze both versions in a requirements.txt (or wherever your repo tracks pinned deps) so your partner doesn't hit the exact same crash when they pull your embed.py changes — this kind of transformers/FlagEmbedding version drift is easy to reintroduce accidentally on a fresh pip install otherwise.

That solves the "make sure my partner doesn't hit the same crash" concern more robustly than a loose requirements.txt, since uv.lock pins the whole dependency tree, not just top-level versions.

uv add -U FlagEmbedding

After either fix, rerun with uv run python scripts/run_ingest.py (or however you're invoking it — uv run ensures it's using the synced environment rather than a stale one) and confirm the BGEM3FlagModel load succeeds past the point it crashed.

8/14/2026 9:11:42

Diagnosed threshold issues and proposed word-level refinements.

Diagnosed threshold issues and proposed word-level refinements.

Done

The threshold isn't really the lever to pull here — the log shows a deeper design mismatch, and fixing that will do more for your WER/CER than any value of conf_thr.

What the log is actually telling you

Look at what triggers vs. what doesn't:

page-009's 48-word paragraph at avg_conf=40.4 triggered the full expensive dual-pass (all 48 words re-OCR'd twice) — and given it's a large block averaging 40% confidence, that's almost certainly a degraded scan region, not mixed-script. Real Bangla text that's just hard to read from a worn page.

Meanwhile, your genuinely mixed-script regions — the plant-usage table with eucalyptus, sugar beet, casuarina, or the caption attributions [ L. S. S. KUMAR ... ] — likely sit inside text blocks that also contain plenty of clean Bangla prose. A region average blends one bad English word in with 50 good Bangla words, so the region-level average often won't drop below threshold at all for the cases you actually built this for.

That's the core problem: you're gating at region granularity, but the actual phenomenon (a garbled English word) is a word-level event. This cuts both ways —

False positives: genuinely degraded Bangla regions (scan noise, not script mismatch) get pulled into the expensive dual-pass, and the swap logic (eng_conf > ben_conf, no margin) will happily replace noisy-but-recognizable Bangla with spurious English garbage whenever Tesseract's eng model scores a garbled glyph shape even slightly higher by chance. That corrupted text then gets masked as "non-Bangla" and skipped by the corrector entirely (since _is_bangla_word() sees Latin characters and protects it from "fixing") — so it ships straight into your chunks uncorrected and wrong. This is very likely your main WER/CER regression source.

False negatives: real mixed-script words buried in otherwise-confident text never trigger the check at all, so the original problem (English force-mapped to Bangla) still isn't fixed for exactly the cases you cared about.

No conf_thr value fixes both problems simultaneously, because the granularity itself is wrong.

Fix: gate per word, not per region

Drop _needs_dual_lang_check() and the region-level average entirely. You already compute per-word confidence in _word_boxes() — use that directly: only re-check individual words that Tesseract's own ben pass was already unsure about, and require a real margin before swapping.

# vision/ocr.py

def _resolve_mixed_script_line(
    img_bgr: np.ndarray, psm: int,
    per_word_conf_thr: float = 55.0,   # only re-check words BEN itself is unsure about
    swap_margin: float = 15.0,          # require ENG to clearly beat BEN, not just edge it out
) -> str:
    """Word-level gate: trust ben's own confidence per word. Only pay for the
    expensive eng re-check on words ben is already unsure about, and only
    accept the eng reading if it's decisively better — not marginally."""
    ben_words = _word_boxes(img_bgr, lang="ben", psm=psm)
    resolved: list[tuple[int, int, str]] = []

    n_rechecked = 0
    for w in ben_words:
        if w["conf"] >= per_word_conf_thr:
            resolved.append((w["line_num"], w["word_num"], w["text"]))
            continue  # ben is already confident — don't second-guess it

        n_rechecked += 1
        x, y, cw, ch = w["left"], w["top"], w["width"], w["height"]
        pad = 2
        crop = img_bgr[max(0, y - pad):y + ch + pad, max(0, x - pad):x + cw + pad]
        if crop.size == 0:
            resolved.append((w["line_num"], w["word_num"], w["text"]))
            continue

        rgb = cv2.cvtColor(crop, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)
        eng_txt = pytesseract.image_to_string(rgb, lang="eng", config="--psm 8").strip()
        eng_data = pytesseract.image_to_data(rgb, lang="eng", config="--psm 8", output_type=pytesseract.Output.DICT)
        eng_conf = max([c for c in eng_data["conf"] if c != -1], default=-1)

        # only swap if eng is DECISIVELY better AND produced real text —
        # a marginal edge is more likely noise than a genuine script fix
        if eng_txt and eng_conf > w["conf"] + swap_margin:
            resolved.append((w["line_num"], w["word_num"], eng_txt))
        else:
            resolved.append((w["line_num"], w["word_num"], w["text"]))  # keep ben's guess either way

    print(f"[ocr] word-level recheck: {n_rechecked}/{len(ben_words)} words re-examined")

    lines: dict[int, list[tuple[int, str]]] = {}
    for line_num, word_num, txt in resolved:
        lines.setdefault(line_num, []).append((word_num, txt))
    out_lines = [" ".join(t for _, t in sorted(words)) for _, words in sorted(lines.items())]
    return "\n".join(out_lines)

And simplify the Pass 1 call site — no more region-level gate, _resolve_mixed_script_line now decides internally which words are worth rechecking:

# Pass 1 loop:
for region in page_regions[key]:
    psm = 7 if region.kind == "heading" else 6
    crop = _crop(image_path, region.bbox)
    raw_text = _resolve_mixed_script_line(crop, psm) if ocr_cfg.get("dual_lang_word_level", True) \
               else _tesseract_region(crop, lang=lang, psm=psm)

Why this should recover (and likely beat) your original numbers:

page-009's 48-word degraded paragraph now only gets the expensive recheck on the individual low-confidence words within it — not the whole block — and each of those swaps requires a 15-point margin, so a garbled Bangla word scoring 40 on ben won't get flipped to English unless eng scores ≥55 on that same crop, which is unlikely for genuinely degraded-but-Bangla content. Much less collateral damage.

Real mixed-script words (a single casuarina sitting in an otherwise-clean sentence) now get checked regardless of the surrounding region's average, since the decision is made per-word using ben's own per-word confidence — this is the case the region-level gate was silently missing.

swap_margin=15 is the actual dial worth tuning (not conf_thr on its own) — raise it toward 20-25 if you still see spurious swaps corrupting genuinely-fine Bangla text; lower it if real English words aren't getting picked up.

How to validate before trusting it on all 749 pages

Rerun your held-out CER/WER (you already track Krishi-Darpan) on the same 13-page sample from this log, and compare three numbers: no dual-lang at all, the old region-gated version, and this word-gated version. That head-to-head is exactly the evidence design_choices.md's OCR row needs — and it'll tell you definitively whether the word-level fix actually recovered the regression, rather than guessing from threshold values alone.

Diagnosed incompatibility between FlagEmbedding and transformers versions.

Done

That confirms it — this is the opposite mismatch from what we assumed. FlagEmbedding==1.4.0 is a fairly recent release that passes dtype to AutoModel.from_pretrained(...), but dtype as a direct kwarg (replacing the older torch_dtype naming) was only added in a later transformers release than 4.44.2. So pinning transformers down to 4.44.2 was actually the wrong direction — I got the causality backwards last time. transformers==4.44.2 predates dtype support entirely, which is exactly why XLMRobertaModel.__init__() doesn't recognize it.

Fix: upgrade transformers to a version that supports dtype, not downgrade it:

uv add "transformers>=4.46,<4.50"
uv sync

I'm giving a range rather than a single pin because I don't have certainty on the exact minimum version that added dtype support — 4.46 is a reasonable floor to try, capped below 4.50 to avoid drifting into whatever future breaking changes might exist there. After this:

uv run python -c "import transformers; print(transformers.__version__)"
uv run python scripts/run_ingest.py

If 4.46 still errors (either the same dtype issue, or a new one), bump the floor up incrementally (4.47, 4.48...) and retry — rather than guessing further, the error message itself will tell you if you've undershot.

One more thing worth checking once this loads: your ocr.py's mT5 corrector also calls AutoModelForSeq2SeqLM.from_pretrained(checkpoint_path, torch_dtype=torch.float32) using the old torch_dtype kwarg name. Newer transformers versions may deprecate (but likely still support, with a warning) torch_dtype — so this shouldn't break, but keep an eye out for a FutureWarning about it, and consider updating that call to dtype=torch.float32 for consistency once you're confident the new transformers version is stable for you.

FlagEmbedding BGEM3FlagModel "unexpected keyword argument 'dtype'" transformers

Done

That first result confirms exactly what's happening: this is a known, documented incompatibility — FlagEmbedding (in your case 1.4.0) hardcodes passing dtype when loading the XLM-RoBERTa backbone, and depending on the exact transformers version, that kwarg either isn't recognized yet or isn't handled the way FlagEmbedding's code expects. We've now tried both directions (down to 4.44.2, up to 4.49.0) and hit the same wall either way — so this isn't a version-window problem, it's FlagEmbedding's wrapper code being fragile against this kwarg across a wide range of transformers releases. That source's fix was blunt but effective: stop going through FlagEmbedding's wrapper, load BGE-M3 via sentence-transformers instead, which handles this kwarg passthrough correctly.

BGE-M3 is officially supported in sentence-transformers, so this isn't a hacky workaround — it's a legitimate alternative path to the same model:

uv add sentence-transformers

Rewrite index/embed.py to drop the FlagEmbedding import entirely:

# index/embed.py
from __future__ import annotations
import numpy as np
from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

_MODEL = None

def _get_model(device: str) -> SentenceTransformer:
    global _MODEL
    if _MODEL is None:
        _MODEL = SentenceTransformer("BAAI/bge-m3", device=device)
    return _MODEL

def encode(chunks: list[Chunk], cfg: dict) -> np.ndarray:
    embed_cfg = cfg.get("embed", {})
    device = cfg.get("device", "cpu")
    batch_size = int(embed_cfg.get("batch_size", 16))

    model = _get_model(device)
    texts = [c.text for c in chunks]

    vectors = model.encode(
        texts,
        batch_size=batch_size,
        normalize_embeddings=True,   # BGE models are trained for cosine sim on normalized vectors
        show_progress_bar=True,
    )
    return np.asarray(vectors, dtype=np.float32)

This produces the same dense 1024-dim BGE-M3 embeddings as before — normalize_embeddings=True matters here since BGE models expect cosine similarity on unit-normalized vectors, so double-check index/store.py's FAISS index type (inner product on normalized vectors ≈ cosine similarity — if it's using IndexFlatIP, you're set; if IndexFlatL2, either switch or keep that in mind).

One tradeoff worth knowing: sentence-transformers' wrapper only exposes BGE-M3's dense output cleanly — the sparse/ColBERT multi-vector modes we discussed earlier as a possible A3 extension are genuinely FlagEmbedding-specific. Since you're already doing dense-only for A2, this costs you nothing now, but worth a line in design_choices.md: "embedding loaded via sentence-transformers instead of FlagEmbedding due to a transformers dtype-kwarg incompatibility; sparse/ColBERT extension would require revisiting this if pursued later."

Run uv sync then rerun run_ingest.py — this sidesteps the broken code path entirely rather than continuing to chase transformers version numbers.

Now for index/store.py — build the FAISS index from your vectors, and persist it so retrieval/retriever.py can load it back without re-embedding.

# index/store.py
from __future__ import annotations
import json
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np
import faiss
from ..contracts import *  # noqa

def _index_dir(cfg: dict) -> Path:
    data_root = Path(cfg.get("ingest", {}).get("data_root", "data"))
    index_cfg = cfg.get("index", {})
    return data_root / index_cfg.get("index_dir", "interim/index")

def build(chunks: list[Chunk], vectors: np.ndarray, cfg: dict) -> None:
    out_dir = _index_dir(cfg)
    out_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    dim = vectors.shape[1]
    index = faiss.IndexFlatIP(dim)   # inner product — matches normalize_embeddings=True in embed.py
    index.add(vectors)

    faiss.write_index(index, str(out_dir / "index.faiss"))

    # persist chunk metadata in the SAME order vectors were added, so a
    # retrieved vector's row index maps directly back to its Chunk
    meta = [c.model_dump() for c in chunks]
    with open(out_dir / "chunks.jsonl", "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        for row in meta:
            f.write(json.dumps(row, ensure_ascii=False) + "\n")

    print(f"[store] wrote {index.ntotal} vectors (dim={dim}) + {len(meta)} chunk records to {out_dir}")

def load(cfg: dict) -> tuple[faiss.Index, list[Chunk]]:
    out_dir = _index_dir(cfg)
    index = faiss.read_index(str(out_dir / "index.faiss"))
    chunks = []
    with open(out_dir / "chunks.jsonl", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        for line in f:
            chunks.append(Chunk.model_validate(json.loads(line)))
    return index, chunks

def search(index: faiss.Index, chunks: list[Chunk], query_vec: np.ndarray, k: int = 20) -> list[Chunk]:
    scores, indices = index.search(query_vec.reshape(1, -1), k)
    results = []
    for score, idx in zip(scores[0], indices[0]):
        if idx == -1:
            continue
        c = chunks[idx]
        c.score = float(score)
        results.append(c)
    return results

A few things worth checking against your actual setup before running:

faiss needs installing — uv add faiss-cpu (use faiss-gpu only if you've confirmed CUDA is set up; faiss-cpu is safer and plenty fast for 749 pages' worth of chunks).

IndexFlatIP matches the normalize_embeddings=True choice we made in embed.py — inner product on unit-normalized vectors is equivalent to cosine similarity. If contracts.py's Chunk doesn't have a mutable score field the way I assumed, adjust search() accordingly (e.g. build a new Chunk copy instead of mutating in place, depending on whether it's a frozen/immutable model).

Chunk-vector ordering must stay in sync — store.build() assumes chunks[i] corresponds to vectors[i], which should hold as long as embed.encode() processes chunks in the same order it receives them (it does, in the version I gave you). Worth a quick sanity print (assert len(chunks) == vectors.shape[0]) right before calling store.build() in pipeline.py, just to catch any accidental reordering early rather than debugging a silent misalignment later.

Once this runs, pipeline.build_knowledge_base() should complete end-to-end — worth running the full run_ingest.py on a larger page slice (not just the debug slice) to catch any remaining issues before moving on to retrieval/retriever.py, which is next.

from future import annotations
"""Stage 3 — OCR/HTR (BASELINE = pretrained TrOCR / Tesseract)

Transcribes page regions detected in Stage 2 using an OCR engine.

Strategy (baseline):

Load OCR model from cfg['ocr']['model'] (e.g., "microsoft/trocr-base-printed")

For each region (except figures), crop the image and run OCR

Return transcribed text as Chunk objects with:
text: extracted transcription

page_ids: list of pages this chunk came from

doc_id: document identifier

Optionally fine-tune on domain-specific data (cfg['ocr']['finetune'])

Reference: EDA §7 "OCR tool bake-off" — tool selection and performance metrics
          EDA §8 "Preprocessing sweep" — per-book preprocessing before OCR
          Config: cfg['ocr']['model'] (HF model ID or path), cfg['ocr']['finetune'] (bool)
"""
"""Stage 3 — OCR/HTR (BASELINE = pretrained foundation, fine-tuned)"""

import re
import time
import unicodedata
from pathlib import Path

import cv2
import numpy as np
import pytesseract
import torch
from jiwer import cer as _cer, wer as _wer
from transformers import AutoModelForSeq2SeqLM, AutoTokenizer
from tqdm import tqdm

from ..contracts import *  # noqa
import json  # add to imports

def _cfg_get(cfg, section, key, default):
    return cfg.get(section, {}).get(key, default)

def _ocr_region_cache_path(ocr_dir: Path, doc_id: str, page_stem: str) -> Path:
    return ocr_dir / "regions" / doc_id / f"{page_stem}.json"

def _save_ocr_regions(path: Path, entries: list[dict]) -> None:
    path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    with open(path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump(entries, f, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)

def _load_ocr_regions(path: Path) -> list[dict]:
    with open(path, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        return json.load(f)

---------------------------------------------------------------------------

Tesseract reading

---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def _crop(image_path: str, bbox: tuple[int, int, int, int]) -> np.ndarray:
    img = cv2.imread(image_path)
    if img is None:
        raise ValueError(f"OpenCV could not read {image_path}")
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = bbox
    h, w = img.shape[:2]
    x1, y1 = max(0, x1), max(0, y1)
    x2, y2 = min(w, x2), min(h, y2)
    return img[y1:y2, x1:x2]

def _tesseract_region(img_bgr: np.ndarray, lang: str, psm: int) -> str:
    rgb = cv2.cvtColor(img_bgr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)
    return pytesseract.image_to_string(rgb, lang=lang, config=f"--psm {psm}").strip()

def _split_lines(text: str) -> list[str]:
    return [ln.strip() for ln in text.splitlines() if ln.strip()]

def _normalize(text: str) -> str:
    text = unicodedata.normalize("NFC", text)
    return re.sub(r"\s+", " ", text).strip()

---------------------------------------------------------------------------

mT5 corrector -- lazy singleton, mirrors vision/layout.py's _get_model()

---------------------------------------------------------------------------

_CORRECTOR = None

class _Corrector:
    def init(self, checkpoint_path: str, max_length: int, num_threads: int | None):
        if num_threads:
            torch.set_num_threads(num_threads)
        start = time.time()
        self.tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(checkpoint_path)
        self.model = AutoModelForSeq2SeqLM.from_pretrained(checkpoint_path, torch_dtype=torch.float32)
        self.model.to("cpu")
        self.model.eval()
        print(f"[timing] mT5 corrector loaded in {time.time() - start:.1f}s "
              f"(one-time cost -- excluded from per-line rate below)")
        self.max_length = max_length
        self.prefix = "ocr_fix: "

@torch.no_grad()
def correct_batch(self, lines: list[str], batch_size: int = 32) -> list[str]:
    if not lines:
        return []
    start = time.time()
    out: list[str] = []
    n_batches = (len(lines) + batch_size - 1) // batch_size
    for i in tqdm(range(0, len(lines), batch_size), total=n_batches, desc="mt5 correct"):
        batch = lines[i:i + batch_size]
        inputs = self.tokenizer(
            [self.prefix + ln for ln in batch],
            return_tensors="pt", truncation=True, padding=True,
            max_length=self.max_length,
        )
        gen_ids = self.model.generate(**inputs, max_length=self.max_length, num_beams=1)
        decoded = self.tokenizer.batch_decode(gen_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)
        out.extend(decoded)
    elapsed = time.time() - start
    rate = len(lines) / elapsed if elapsed > 0 else float("inf")
    print(f"[timing] correction: {len(lines)} lines in {elapsed:.1f}s ({rate:.1f} lines/sec)")
    return out

def _get_corrector(cfg: dict) -> "_Corrector":
    global _CORRECTOR
    if _CORRECTOR is None:
        checkpoint_path = cfg["ocr"]["corrector_checkpoint_path"]
        max_length = int(_cfg_get(cfg, "ocr", "corrector_max_length", 96))
        num_threads = _cfg_get(cfg, "ocr", "corrector_cpu_threads", None)
        _CORRECTOR = _Corrector(checkpoint_path, max_length, num_threads)
    return _CORRECTOR

---------------------------------------------------------------------------

Ground-truth lookup -- used ONLY as the reference string for scoring, never

to decide correspondence between predicted and reference text

---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def _parse_page_id(page_id: str) -> tuple[str, str]:
    """loader.py builds Page.id as ':' -- split it back
    apart to locate the source image and the matching GT file."""
    doc_id, _, page_stem = page_id.partition(":")
    return doc_id, page_stem

def _load_gt_lines(gt_root: Path, doc_id: str, page_stem: str) -> list[str] | None:
    gt_path = gt_root / doc_id / f"{page_stem}.txt"
    if not gt_path.exists():
        return None
    with open(gt_path, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        return _split_lines(f.read())

def _region_chunk_id(region: Region) -> str:
    """Deterministic id from page_id + bbox -- stable across re-runs on the
    same layout output, since Region carries no id of its own."""
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = region.bbox
    return f"{region.page_id}:{x1}-{y1}-{x2}-{y2}"

class _MetricAccumulator:
    """Accumulates one (hypothesis, reference) pair PER PAGE -- not per line
    and not via any GT-informed matching. Each pair is: the system's full
    output for that page (regions concatenated in bbox reading order) vs.
    that page's full GT text. jiwer's own edit-distance computation is the
    only alignment performed, and it never sees the other side's identity
    while deciding correspondence -- it just diffs two fixed strings."""
    def init(self, held_out_docs: set[str]):
        self.held_out_docs = held_out_docs
        self.all_hyp, self.all_ref = [], []
        self.held_hyp, self.held_ref = [], []

def add(self, doc_id: str, hyp: str, ref: str):
    if not ref.strip():
        return
    self.all_hyp.append(hyp); self.all_ref.append(ref)
    if doc_id in self.held_out_docs:
        self.held_hyp.append(hyp); self.held_ref.append(ref)

def report(self, label: str):
    def _scores(hyps, refs):
        return (_cer(refs, hyps), _wer(refs, hyps)) if refs else None
    corpus, held = _scores(self.all_hyp, self.all_ref), _scores(self.held_hyp, self.held_ref)
    print(f"\n=== {label} ===")
    print(f"corpus-wide : cer={corpus[0]:.4f}  wer={corpus[1]:.4f}  (n={len(self.all_ref)} pages)"
          if corpus else "corpus-wide : no ground truth found -- skipped")
    print(f"held-out    : cer={held[0]:.4f}  wer={held[1]:.4f}  (n={len(self.held_ref)} pages)"
          if held else "held-out    : no ground truth found -- skipped")

---------------------------------------------------------------------------

Main entry point

---------------------------------------------------------------------------

class Reader:
    """Kept for interface compatibility -- transcribe() below does the real work."""
    def init(self, cfg: dict) -> None:
        self.cfg = cfg["ocr"]

def transcribe_region(self, region: Region) -> str:
    raise NotImplementedError("use transcribe()")

def transcribe(regions: list[Region], cfg: dict) -> list[Chunk]:
    ocr_cfg = cfg.get("ocr", {})
    lang = ocr_cfg.get("tesseract_lang", "ben")
    use_corrector = ocr_cfg.get("use_corrector", True)
    batch_size = int(ocr_cfg.get("corrector_batch_size", 32))
    data_root = Path(_cfg_get(cfg, "ingest", "data_root", "data"))
    pages_dir = _cfg_get(cfg, "ingest", "pages_dir", "pages")
    ocr_dir = data_root / _cfg_get(cfg, "ingest", "ocr_dir", "interim/ocr")
    gt_root = data_root / ocr_cfg.get("gt_dir", "ground-truth-ocr")
    held_out_docs = set(ocr_cfg.get("held_out_docs", ["Krishi-Darpan"]))
    is_debug = bool(cfg.get("debug"))

raw_metrics = _MetricAccumulator(held_out_docs)
corrected_metrics = _MetricAccumulator(held_out_docs) if use_corrector else None

all_input_regions = regions
regions = sorted([r for r in regions if r.kind != "figure"],
                  key=lambda r: (r.page_id, r.bbox[1], r.bbox[0]))

page_regions: dict[tuple[str, str], list[Region]] = {}
for region in regions:
    doc_id, page_stem = _parse_page_id(region.page_id)
    page_regions.setdefault((doc_id, page_stem), []).append(region)

# (page_key -> list of {"region", "raw_text", "corrected_text"}), sourced from
# EITHER cache or fresh OCR below -- scoring and Chunk emission don't care which.
page_entries: dict[tuple[str, str], list[dict]] = {}
pages_to_process: list[tuple[str, str]] = []

for key in page_regions:
    doc_id, page_stem = key
    cache_path = _ocr_region_cache_path(ocr_dir, doc_id, page_stem)
    if cache_path.exists():
        page_entries[key] = _load_ocr_regions(cache_path)
    else:
        pages_to_process.append(key)

# Pass 1: Tesseract per region, only for uncached pages.
region_raw_lines: dict[tuple[str, str], list[list[str]]] = {}
flat_lines: list[str] = []
flat_owner: list[tuple[tuple[str, str], int, int]] = []
t_pass1 = time.time()
for key in tqdm(pages_to_process, desc="tesseract"):
    doc_id, page_stem = key
    image_path = str(data_root / pages_dir / doc_id / f"{page_stem}.png")
    per_region_lines: list[list[str]] = []
    for region in page_regions[key]:
        psm = 7 if region.kind == "heading" else 6
        crop = _crop(image_path, region.bbox)
        raw_text = _tesseract_region(crop, lang=lang, psm=psm)
        lines = [_normalize(ln) for ln in _split_lines(raw_text)]
        region_idx = len(per_region_lines)
        per_region_lines.append(lines)
        for j, ln in enumerate(lines):
            flat_lines.append(ln)
            flat_owner.append((key, region_idx, j))
    region_raw_lines[key] = per_region_lines
print(f"[timing] tesseract pass: {len(pages_to_process)} pages, "
      f"{len(flat_lines)} lines in {time.time() - t_pass1:.1f}s")

# Pass 2: batched correction over every freshly-OCR'd line.
corrected_flat = _get_corrector(cfg).correct_batch(flat_lines, batch_size=batch_size) \
    if use_corrector and flat_lines else flat_lines

region_corrected_lines = {k: [list(lines) for lines in v] for k, v in region_raw_lines.items()}
for (key, region_idx, line_idx), corrected in zip(flat_owner, corrected_flat):
    region_corrected_lines[key][region_idx][line_idx] = corrected

# Pass 2.5: build region entries for freshly-processed pages, write region cache.
for key in pages_to_process:
    doc_id, page_stem = key
    entries = []
    for region_idx, region in enumerate(page_regions[key]):
        raw_text = "\n".join(region_raw_lines[key][region_idx])
        corrected_text = "\n".join(region_corrected_lines[key][region_idx]) if use_corrector else raw_text
        entries.append({
            "region": region.model_dump(),
            "raw_text": raw_text,
            "corrected_text": corrected_text,
        })
    page_entries[key] = entries
    if not is_debug:
        _save_ocr_regions(_ocr_region_cache_path(ocr_dir, doc_id, page_stem), entries)

# Pass 3: page-level scoring -- concatenate each page's cached/fresh region
# entries in list order (== spatial order, preserved by the cache) and diff
# against GT text. Cache now carries both raw and corrected text per region,
# so raw_metrics is scoreable on cache-hit pages too.
for key, entries in page_entries.items():
    doc_id, page_stem = key
    gt_lines = _load_gt_lines(gt_root, doc_id, page_stem)
    if not gt_lines:
        continue
    page_gt_text = "\n".join(_normalize(g) for g in gt_lines)
    page_raw_text = "\n".join(e["raw_text"] for e in entries)
    page_corrected_text = "\n".join(e["corrected_text"] for e in entries)
    raw_metrics.add(doc_id, page_raw_text, page_gt_text)
    if corrected_metrics is not None:
        corrected_metrics.add(doc_id, page_corrected_text, page_gt_text)

# Pass 4: one Chunk PER REGION, built uniformly from page_entries regardless
# of cache vs. fresh-OCR source.
chunks: list[Chunk] = []
kind_counts: dict[str, int] = {}
for entries in page_entries.values():
    for e in entries:
        region = Region.model_validate(e["region"])
        kind_counts[region.kind] = kind_counts.get(region.kind, 0) + 1
        chunks.append(Chunk(
            id=_region_chunk_id(region),
            doc_id=_parse_page_id(region.page_id)[0],
            text=e["corrected_text"] if use_corrector else e["raw_text"],
            page_ids=[region.page_id],
            score=0.0,
        ))

skipped_figures = sum(1 for r in all_input_regions if r.kind == "figure")

print(f"\n=== Region accounting ===")
print(f"input regions by kind (chunked) : {kind_counts}")
print(f"figures skipped (no text to extract) : {skipped_figures}")
print(f"pages cached (skipped OCR) : {len(page_entries) - len(pages_to_process)}")
print(f"pages processed this run   : {len(pages_to_process)}")
print(f"chunks emitted : {len(chunks)}")

raw_metrics.report("Tesseract (raw)")
if corrected_metrics is not None:
    corrected_metrics.report("Tesseract + mT5 corrector")

return chunks
